# New SIWES Placement — Complete Chat Summary

## Context

* This is a **new SIWES placement at a steel company**.
* The user is documenting their experience here so the information can later be converted into **SIWES logbook entries**.
* The user does **not necessarily record experiences in chronological order**, so individual observations should be kept separately until they request compilation.
* The user wants help understanding unfamiliar technical terms and identifying what IT-related topics they should learn more about from what they observe.

## Day 1 / Initial Orientation

* The user attended a **safety induction**.
* The induction introduced workplace safety and emergency-related procedures.
* The user was not initially engaged much because staff were busy.
* The user eventually got to the assigned department on the following day.

## Day 2 / Getting to the Department

* The user finally got to the department at around **2:00 PM**.
* Staff members had been too busy to engage the user extensively.
* The user therefore began learning through **observation and listening to conversations**.
* The user started picking up unfamiliar technical words from what staff were discussing.
* The **HOD (Head of Department)** eventually engaged the user.
* The HOD asked about:

  * The user's college/school.
  * Accommodation.
  * Whether getting to the workplace from home was easy.
* This served as an initial introduction and interaction with the department.

## Fire Safety and Emergency Systems

* The company/department is preparing for what the user described as an **IT audit related to fire emergency/safety systems**.
* They are installing a **digital communication fire alarm**.
* Staff discussed **fixing/servicing fire extinguishers**.
* The user observed staff looking for **specifications** for the fire alarm system.
* Staff also discussed **batteries/power requirements** for the fire alarm.
* The user heard something that sounded like **2.7 W**, but this was not confirmed.
* It was explained that **watts (W) measure power**, not battery capacity. Battery specifications are more commonly expressed using **volts (V)** and **amp-hours (Ah)** or **milliamp-hours (mAh)**.
* The user also saw/heard about a **smoke detector**.
* A smoke detector detects smoke particles and provides an early warning of fire.
* Smoke detectors can be connected to a fire alarm/control system.
* The fire alarm system can communicate alarm conditions to a central control system.

## IT and Fire Safety

* It was explained that IT in an industrial company can interact with physical safety systems.
* Digital fire alarm systems can involve:

  * Digital communication.
  * Monitoring.
  * Networking.
  * Configuration.
  * Technical infrastructure.
* The fire alarm itself is primarily a safety system, but its digital communication and integration can overlap with IT/technical infrastructure.
* The user is therefore seeing an example of **IT interacting with physical/industrial systems**.

## IT Server Room

* The user saw a book/label that said **“IT Server Room.”**
* A server room is a controlled area where critical IT equipment is kept.
* Possible equipment includes:

  * Servers.
  * Network switches.
  * Routers.
  * Storage systems.
  * Monitoring equipment.
  * UPS/power backup equipment.
* The user observed **monitors, CPUs/computers**, and systems that appear to remain switched on continuously.
* It was explained that some enterprise systems remain powered on because they provide services that need to operate continuously, such as:

  * Network services.
  * Data storage.
  * Monitoring.
  * Security systems.
  * Business applications.
  * Communication between systems.

## “Blue Gate” / UPS

* The user noticed something called **“Blue Gate”** and asked whether it was a UPS.
* Its identity was not confirmed, so it should not be recorded as definitely being a UPS without checking.
* A **UPS (Uninterruptible Power Supply)** provides temporary backup power and can protect IT equipment from certain power disturbances.
* A UPS can help keep important systems running during a power outage or provide time for systems to shut down safely.
* In an enterprise environment, UPS systems can work alongside generators or other backup power systems.

## VirtualBox

* The user saw another IT student learning how to install **VirtualBox**.
* VirtualBox is virtualization software.
* It allows a physical computer to run **virtual machines (VMs)**.
* A virtual machine behaves like a separate computer inside the physical computer.
* VirtualBox can be used to:

  * Run another operating system.
  * Test software.
  * Practice server administration.
  * Practice networking.
  * Create virtual servers.
  * Build cybersecurity labs.
* Virtualization is therefore relevant to systems administration, networking, cybersecurity, and server infrastructure.

## VRF

* The user initially wondered whether a term was **“VPF”**, then clarified that it was **VRF in relation to IP**.
* The relevant meaning is:

  * **VRF = Virtual Routing and Forwarding.**
* VRF is a networking technology.
* It allows one physical router or Layer 3 device to maintain **multiple separate routing tables**.
* A simple way to understand it is:

  * One physical router can behave like several independent routers.
* VRFs can be used to separate networks or departments.
* They can provide:

  * Network isolation.
  * Better organization.
  * Security through separation.
  * Independent routing environments.
* VRF is directly related to:

  * IP addressing.
  * Routing.
  * Routers.
  * Layer 3 networking.
  * Network segmentation.
* The user was also informed that **VRF can have another meaning in HVAC**:

  * Variable Refrigerant Flow.
* However, because the user encountered VRF specifically in relation to **IP/networking**, the relevant meaning for their SIWES learning is **Virtual Routing and Forwarding**.
* The user does not need to master VRF immediately; understanding the basic concept of **separating different routing environments/networks** is enough at this stage.

## VNC

* The user added **VNC** to the list of technical terms they want to understand.
* **VNC = Virtual Network Computing.**
* VNC allows a person to remotely access and control another computer through a network.
* It can be used for:

  * Remote technical support.
  * Server access.
  * Troubleshooting.
  * Managing computers remotely.
* VNC is therefore connected to networking, remote administration, and IT support.

## Field Work

* The user asked what **field work** means in this environment.
* It was explained that field work in IT can involve practical, physical activities outside the normal desk environment.
* In a steel/industrial company, IT field work may include:

  * Network cabling.
  * Running Ethernet cables.
  * Connecting network points.
  * Installing or replacing cables.
  * Connecting switches and other devices.
  * Testing network connections.
  * Cable labeling.
  * Cable management.
  * Access point installation.
  * Physical troubleshooting.
* **IT/network cabling is an IT activity**, particularly when it forms part of the organization's communication/network infrastructure.
* Field work does not mean the work is outside IT; it is a practical aspect of IT infrastructure and networking.

## Industrial IT Environment

* The user clarified that the company is a **steel company**.
* This means the IT environment is an **industrial IT environment**, rather than an ordinary office-only environment.
* IT in a steel company may support:

  * Office computers.
  * Network infrastructure.
  * Servers.
  * Communication systems.
  * Monitoring systems.
  * Security systems.
  * Emergency/safety systems.
  * Production-related systems.
  * Data and information systems.
* IT therefore extends beyond simply repairing computers.

## IT Areas the User Should Learn

Based on the user's observations, the most useful areas to study are:

### Networking

## Main Learning Strategy

The user does not need to master every unfamiliar technology immediately.

A useful approach is to identify:

* **What is this?**
* **What does it do?**
* **Why is the company using it?**
* **What does it connect to?**
* **What IT concept does it relate to?**

The user can continue sending unfamiliar terms or observations as they encounter them, and they can be explained and added to the accumulated SIWES knowledge record.

## Technical Terms Collected

* **IT** — Information Technology.
* **IT Infrastructure** — The hardware, software, networks, servers, and supporting systems used by an organization.
* **Server** — A computer/system that provides services or resources to other computers.
* **Server Room** — A controlled area containing critical IT infrastructure.
* **UPS** — Uninterruptible Power Supply; provides temporary backup power and protection for connected equipment.
* **VRF** — Virtual Routing and Forwarding; allows separate routing environments on the same physical networking device.
* **VNC** — Virtual Network Computing; allows remote access and control of a computer over a network.
* **VirtualBox** — Virtualization software used to create and run virtual machines.
* **VM** — Virtual Machine; a software-based computer running within a physical computer.
* **IP Address** — An address used to identify a device/interface on an IP network.
* **Network Cabling** — Physical cabling used to connect network devices and endpoints.
* **Smoke Detector** — A device that detects smoke and provides an early warning of fire.
* **Digital Fire Alarm** — A fire alarm system capable of electronically communicating information from detectors/devices to a control system.
* **IT Audit** — An examination/check of IT systems, controls, processes, and related requirements; in this context, the exact scope of the audit should be confirmed.
* **Field Work** — Practical work carried out in the physical operational environment, such as cabling, installations, and troubleshooting.
* **PPE** — Personal Protective Equipment, relevant to safe work in an industrial environment.

# SIWES Day 3 — Comprehensive Knowledge & Experience Record

## 1. Crimping Tools and RJ45

I learned about **crimping tools**, particularly their use with Ethernet/network cables.

### Crimping

* A crimping tool is used to attach/terminate an **RJ45 connector** to an Ethernet cable.
* The process involves:

  1. Cutting the Ethernet cable to the required length.
  2. Stripping the outer insulation to expose the eight internal wires.
  3. Untwisting and arranging the wires according to the required wiring standard.
  4. Trimming the wires evenly.
  5. Inserting the wires into an RJ45 connector.
  6. Making sure the wires reach the end of the connector and the cable jacket is properly positioned.
  7. Placing the connector in the crimping tool and pressing it firmly.
  8. Testing the completed cable with a cable tester or by connecting it to a network device.

### T568B colour arrangement

The common T568B sequence discussed was:

1. White/Orange
2. Orange
3. White/Green
4. Blue
5. White/Blue
6. Green
7. White/Brown
8. Brown

A memory pattern was developed:

**Orange → Green → Blue → Green → Brown**

Or:

**OO | G | BB | G | BB**

The important idea is that the orange pair comes first, the blue pair is in the middle, and the brown pair is last, while the green pair is split around the blue pair.

### Relationship with CCTV

I learned that Ethernet cables and RJ45 connectors can be used with **IP CCTV cameras**.

A typical IP CCTV connection can involve:

**Camera → Ethernet cable → PoE switch/NVR → Network/monitoring system**

The Ethernet cable can carry network data and, when PoE is used, power as well.

Therefore, incorrect crimping or a damaged Ethernet connection can cause:

* Camera to go offline
* Loss of video
* Network communication failure
* Camera power/PoE problems

This helped connect the practical skill of crimping to actual CCTV installation and troubleshooting.

# 8. Logic Gates and Real-Life Applications

I also learned about the practical idea of **logic gates** and how logical conditions can be applied to physical systems such as doors and light switches.

The basic concepts discussed were:

### AND

All required conditions must be satisfied.

**AND = both/all conditions**

### OR

Any one of the conditions can be satisfied.

**OR = either/any condition**

### NOT

The output is the opposite of the input.

**NOT = reverse the condition**

I understood the door examples using a **regular key door**, rather than an electronic/maglock door.

The idea I wanted to remember was:

### OR door

A single regular-key door that can be locked/unlocked from the inside or outside.

The idea of OR helped me understand a situation where **either side can provide the required action**.

### AND door

A door where one side can be locked and the other side can also be locked, meaning that **to completely lock the door, both sides must be locked**.

This particular everyday example made the logic-gate concept easier for me to understand.

# 14. Learning From Experienced IT Professionals

One of the biggest things I learned was that **you cannot know everything**.

I noticed that people who obviously have considerable technical knowledge still:

* Search for information
* Refer to documentation
* Watch YouTube videos
* Use Udemy courses
* Use AI for ideas/help
* Research unfamiliar problems
* Take additional professional courses

I also spoke with one person who had **dabbled in many different areas** and was still taking online and professional courses.

This changed my understanding of what being knowledgeable in IT means.

### Important lesson:

An experienced IT professional does not necessarily have every answer memorized.

Instead, they know how to:

* Identify what they don't know
* Find reliable information
* Research efficiently
* Learn what is required
* Apply the information
* Troubleshoot
* Continue developing their skills

**Expertise is not knowing everything; it includes knowing how to find, understand and apply what you need.**

# 15. Learning to Think Through Different Scenarios

I noticed that my supervisor seems able to think through many possible scenarios whenever he is given a task.

He appears to consider:

* What could go wrong?
* What could cause the problem?
* What if one component fails?
* What alternative explanation could exist?
* What should be checked first?
* What should be checked next?

I wanted to know how to develop this ability.

The key idea is that this ability comes largely from **experience and pattern recognition**, rather than simply being born with it.

A useful method is:

**System → Components → Possible failures → Tests/checks → Solution**

For example, when looking at a CCTV system, instead of seeing only “camera not working,” think about:

* Camera
* Cable
* RJ45 connector
* Power/PoE
* Switch
* Network
* Configuration
* NVR/software

Then ask:

**“Which of these could be responsible for the failure?”**

The goal is to gradually develop the habit of thinking about possible failure points before immediately attempting a fix.

---

# 16. Observing Experts More Actively

Another lesson was that simply watching an experienced technician is not enough.

A better approach is to:

* Watch what they do.
* Predict what they will check next.
* Think about why they chose that step.
* Compare my prediction with what they actually did.
* Ask questions when something is unclear.
* Remember the problem and solution.

This should gradually build a mental library of common IT problems and solutions.

* Continuous learning is normal even for people with years of experience.
* My supervisor's ability to anticipate problems is something I can develop through deliberate observation, troubleshooting practice and repeated exposure to real systems.

Day 3 exposed me to a much wider range of real-world IT work than just computer repair. I encountered networking and Ethernet cabling, CCTV installation and monitoring, switch configuration, technical documentation, access control, AutoCAD, enterprise communication technology, procurement documentation, virtualization and general IT support.

My goal should therefore not be to memorize every possible IT concept. I should build strong fundamentals, gain practical experience, learn how systems work, and develop the ability to **research, troubleshoot and reason through unfamiliar problems**.

# 4. Ping / “Reply from IP address, bytes, TTL”

### What you observed

You said the person working with the switch was working on something that displayed something similar to:

> `Reply from 'IP address' bytes=22 TTL=63`

in a terminal.

### Explanation given

This is typical output from the **`ping` command**.

Ping is a network troubleshooting/diagnostic utility used to test whether another device can be reached over a network.

A simplified process is:

**Computer → network → destination IP → response**

If the destination responds, the terminal may display:

* `Reply from ...`
* The destination IP address
* Number of bytes
* TTL
* Usually response time as well

### Meaning of the components

**Reply from IP address**

* Indicates that the target device responded to the ping request.
* This generally means the destination is reachable through the network path being tested.

**Bytes**

* Indicates the size of the ICMP response packet being reported.

**TTL**

* Stands for **Time To Live**.
* It is a field in the IP packet that prevents packets from circulating indefinitely.
* It is decreased as a packet passes through Layer 3 routing devices/hops.
* The value can provide clues about the path, but it is **not simply a direct measurement of the number of switches between two devices**.

The earlier explanation simplified TTL as “how many hops the packet can still pass through.” More precisely, TTL is an IP field that is decremented by routers/hops.

### What the person's activity could have been

Based only on what you saw, the safest interpretation is that he was likely:

* Testing network connectivity.
* Checking whether a device/IP address was reachable.
* Troubleshooting a network connection.
* Verifying network configuration/connectivity.

But we cannot know exactly what problem he was solving just from the ping output.

---

# 5. How a switch relates to ping

You asked for both:

* What the switch is doing during a ping.
* How VLANs affect ping.

### Switch and ping

A switch primarily operates at **Layer 2**, although Layer 3 switches can also perform routing.

When a device sends traffic to another device on the same local network:

1. The source device creates the network traffic.
2. The traffic reaches the switch.
3. The switch uses its **MAC address table** to determine where to forward Ethernet frames.
4. The switch forwards the frame through the appropriate port.

If communication needs to go to a **different IP network**, routing is required.

That routing can be performed by:

* A router
* A Layer 3 switch

The **default gateway** is commonly the device responsible for forwarding traffic outside the local subnet.

---

# 6. VLANs

### What you initially observed

You thought the staff member's work involved something related to **VLANs**, but later clarified that you weren't certain exactly what he was doing.

So VLAN should be treated as a **networking concept you encountered**, rather than something you personally configured that day.

### Explanation given

**VLAN = Virtual Local Area Network.**

A VLAN allows one physical switching infrastructure to be logically divided into separate networks.

Example:

* VLAN 10 → Administration
* VLAN 20 → Engineering
* VLAN 30 → Guests

These can exist on the same physical switch while remaining logically separated.

### VLAN and ping

If two devices are in the **same VLAN/subnet**, they can generally communicate directly through the switch, assuming other configurations allow it.

If two devices are in **different VLANs**, they normally cannot communicate directly at Layer 2.

Communication between VLANs requires **inter-VLAN routing**, usually through:

* A router
* A Layer 3 switch

So:

**Same VLAN → switching**

**Different VLANs → routing required**

This connects directly to the networking concepts you are likely to encounter in Cisco training.

### Explanation given

**ODBC = Open Database Connectivity.**

It is a standard interface that allows applications to communicate with databases through appropriate drivers.

A simplified model is:

**Application → ODBC → ODBC Driver → Database**

For example, an application may use an ODBC driver to communicate with a particular database system.

### Important distinction

ODBC is **not a database**.

It is a method/interface for enabling software to communicate with databases.

It can be relevant to:

* Enterprise applications
* Reporting systems
* Business software
* Data analysis
* Database-connected applications

### 2. Desktop with no HDMI display

Another activity involved troubleshooting a **desktop computer that was not displaying through HDMI**.

What was observed:

* The desktop was powered on but was not producing display through HDMI.
* It was established that the **HDMI cable and HDMI port were not the problem**.
* The technicians therefore investigated the internal hardware.
* They removed the **RAM (Random Access Memory)** from the system unit.
* The RAM was cleaned and then used/reseated in the system.
* After addressing the RAM, the display problem was resolved.

Technical lesson discussed:

* A RAM problem can cause a computer to power on while failing to produce a display.
* Removing, cleaning, and properly reseating RAM can resolve issues caused by poor contact, dust, or improper seating.
* The troubleshooting demonstrated the importance of checking internal hardware when external display components have already been ruled out.

---

### 3. Laptop RAM: onboard and removable RAM

The user also observed technicians opening a **laptop to inspect its RAM** because the laptop had an issue.

During this, the user encountered the concepts of:

* **Onboard RAM** — memory chips soldered directly onto the motherboard. It generally cannot be removed or upgraded separately.
* **Removable RAM** — a RAM module installed in a RAM slot, which can normally be removed, replaced, or upgraded.

A laptop **can have both onboard and removable RAM simultaneously**. For example, a laptop could have some RAM soldered to the motherboard and an additional removable RAM module in a slot.

The practical distinction discussed:

* If a removable RAM module develops a fault, it can usually be replaced.
* If onboard RAM develops a fault, the problem is more difficult because the memory is integrated into the motherboard.

---

### 4. LAN/Ethernet cable crimping

The user was also shown how to **crimp a LAN/Ethernet cable using an RJ45 connector**.

The cable was initially described as “Cat 4,” but the user clarified that it was probably **Cat 5 or Cat 6**. The exact category was not confirmed. It was explained that the cable jacket normally has the category printed on it, such as **CAT5e** or **CAT6**.

Equipment/components involved:

* Ethernet/LAN cable
* **RJ45 connector**
* Crimping tool

The user remembered the wire colour arrangement as:

* Striped orange → Orange
* Striped green → Blue
* Striped blue → Green
* Striped brown → Brown

This was identified as the **T568B wiring arrangement**, whose complete order from left to right is:

1. White/Orange
2. Orange
3. White/Green
4. Blue
5. White/Blue
6. Green
7. White/Brown
8. Brown

The crimping process discussed:

1. Strip the outer insulation of the Ethernet cable.
2. Untwist and straighten the individual wires.
3. Arrange the eight wires according to the T568B colour sequence.
4. Insert the wires into the RJ45 connector.
5. Crimp the connector using a crimping tool.
6. Test the completed cable, typically with a LAN cable tester.

This was identified as practical **networking experience**, applicable to office LANs, switches, routers, computers, and CCTV/network installations.

Formatting would remove the existing operating-system installation and allow a fresh Windows installation, but it is normally treated as a last resort because of its impact on the existing data and software.

## SIWES Day 8 — Full Information Summary

**Date/Day:** Day 8, Week 2, Wednesday

### Activities and observations

* Observed a **dual-monitor setup** in which two monitors were being used together as one extended display/workspace.
* Mr. Israel explained how the monitors were connected and configured.
* Initially theorized that one monitor was connected through **HDMI** while the other was connected through **VGA**, but this was only an assumption and may not have been the actual configuration.
* Learned that a computer can potentially support **more than two monitors**, such as adding a third monitor, depending on the available display outputs and the graphics hardware.
* Learned that common display interfaces include **HDMI, VGA, DisplayPort and DVI**, and that the number of monitors a system can support depends largely on the computer's graphics capabilities and available outputs.
* Learned about different Windows multi-display modes:

  * **Duplicate:** displays the same content on all connected monitors.
  * **Extend:** allows the monitors to function as one larger workspace.
  * **Second screen only:** displays content only on the external monitor.
* Learned that **Windows + P** can be used to quickly access the Windows display projection options.

### Operating system installation / formatting

* Gained an introductory understanding of how to **format a system unit and install a new operating system**.
* Learned that a **bootable USB flash drive** can be used to start the computer and install an operating system.
* Understood the general process as:

  1. Prepare/create a bootable USB containing the OS installation files.
  2. Insert the USB into the computer.
  3. Access the computer's **BIOS/UEFI or Boot Menu**.
  4. Select the USB as the boot device.
  5. Boot from the USB.
  6. Format/partition the appropriate drive when required.
  7. Install the new operating system.
* Learned that BIOS/UEFI controls important hardware and boot-related settings before the operating system starts.
* Common keys for accessing BIOS/UEFI or boot menus include **F2, Delete, F10, F12 and Esc**, although the exact key depends on the computer manufacturer and model.
* Learned that **F10 is commonly used on HP systems**, while F12 is commonly used for a boot-device menu on many systems.
* Learned that the relevant key usually needs to be pressed repeatedly immediately after powering on the computer.

### BIOS/UEFI concepts learned

* **Boot Order/Boot Priority:** determines which device the computer attempts to boot from first. A bootable USB may need to be selected or moved to the appropriate position.
* **UEFI:** the modern firmware interface used by most newer computers.
* **Legacy BIOS/CSM:** an older compatibility boot mode used by older systems or certain installation configurations.
* **Secure Boot:** a security feature that helps ensure only trusted boot software is loaded. It may sometimes need to be disabled for particular OS installations, although it should not be disabled unnecessarily.
* **GPT and MBR:** disk partitioning schemes associated with different boot configurations. Modern UEFI installations generally use **GPT**, while older Legacy BIOS installations commonly use **MBR**.

### Important clarification from the learning

The initial assumption that the two monitors were simply working because **one used HDMI and the other used VGA** is not necessarily correct. Multiple monitors can be connected through different combinations of display outputs, provided the computer's graphics hardware and ports support the required displays. Therefore, the more accurate lesson is that **multi-monitor capability depends on the graphics hardware, available display outputs, and system configuration—not simply on using different cable types.**

Day 8 introduced practical concepts in **computer hardware configuration, display technology, BIOS/UEFI, bootable media, system formatting, and operating-system installation**. The day also highlighted the importance of distinguishing between what was directly observed and what was only assumed, particularly regarding the monitor connections.

Below is a comprehensive, copyable summary of everything discussed for **SIWES Day 8**, including your observations and the technical explanations we covered.

SIWES DAY 8 – COMPREHENSIVE SUMMARY

On SIWES Day 8, I learned about preparing a USB flash drive as a bootable device for operating system installation and system recovery. I was introduced to the different boot modes, partitioning systems, bootable USB creation tools, virtualization concepts, and important computer specifications to consider before formatting or installing an operating system.

1. CREATING A BOOTABLE USB

I learned how to convert a USB flash drive into a bootable device. A bootable USB contains the necessary boot files and operating system installation files required for a computer to start from the USB instead of its internal storage.

Rufus was introduced as a commonly used application for creating bootable USB drives. An ISO image of an operating system can be selected in Rufus and written to the USB drive. During the process, different options such as the partition scheme and target system/boot mode may need to be selected.

Creating a bootable USB is useful for:

* Installing Windows or Linux.
* Reinstalling an operating system.
* Repairing or troubleshooting a computer that cannot boot normally.
* Performing system recovery or maintenance.

2. BIOS/LEGACY AND UEFI BOOT MODES

I learned about Legacy BIOS and UEFI, which are firmware interfaces responsible for initializing computer hardware and starting the operating system.

Legacy BIOS is the older boot method. It is commonly associated with the MBR partitioning system.

UEFI (Unified Extensible Firmware Interface) is the modern replacement for traditional BIOS. It supports newer technologies such as GPT partitioning and Secure Boot and is generally more suitable for modern computers.

I also learned about:

* UEFI Non-CSM: The computer operates purely in UEFI mode and does not provide compatibility with legacy BIOS booting.
* UEFI with CSM: CSM means Compatibility Support Module. It allows a UEFI-based system to support older Legacy BIOS-style booting, which can be useful when dealing with older operating systems or MBR-partitioned drives.

3. RUFUS

Rufus is a utility used to create bootable USB flash drives. When creating a bootable USB, Rufus can provide options such as the ISO file to use, partition scheme, and target boot system.

The choice between GPT and MBR is important because it should generally correspond with the system's intended boot mode.

4. MBR AND GPT PARTITIONING

I learned about two major disk partitioning systems:

MBR (Master Boot Record):

* An older partitioning system.
* Commonly associated with Legacy BIOS booting.
* Has limitations compared with GPT, including limitations on partition sizes and the number of primary partitions.

GPT (GUID Partition Table):

* A modern partitioning system.
* Designed to work particularly well with UEFI systems.
* Supports very large storage devices and more partitions than traditional MBR.
* Is generally preferred for modern computers.

Therefore, when preparing a bootable USB or installing an operating system, the partition scheme and boot mode need to be compatible.

5. VIRTUALIZATION AND VMWARE

I was also introduced to virtualization and VMware. Virtualization allows a physical computer to run one or more virtual computers, called virtual machines (VMs), using software.

I learned about two major types of hypervisors:

Type 1 Hypervisor (Bare-Metal Hypervisor):

* Runs directly on the physical computer's hardware.
* Does not require a conventional host operating system underneath it.
* Commonly used in servers and enterprise environments.
* An example is VMware ESXi.

Type 2 Hypervisor (Hosted Hypervisor):

* Runs as an application on top of an existing operating system.
* Commonly used on normal PCs for testing, learning, development, and running virtual machines.
* Examples include VMware Workstation and VirtualBox.

The basic distinction is that Type 1 runs directly on the hardware, while Type 2 runs on top of a host operating system.

6. COMPUTER GENERATIONS AND PROCESSORS

I heard the term "14th generation," referring to Intel 14th-generation processors. Processor generation is one factor that can help indicate how modern a processor is, although generation alone does not determine overall performance.

When evaluating a computer, it is important to look beyond the generation number and examine the processor model, cores, threads, clock speeds, architecture, and other specifications.

7. COMPUTER SPECIFICATIONS TO CONSIDER BEFORE FORMATTING

I learned that several hardware specifications should be considered before formatting a computer or installing an operating system.

RAM (Random Access Memory):
RAM is the computer's working memory. It temporarily holds data and programs that are actively being used. More RAM generally allows a system to handle more applications and multitasking more comfortably.

Storage:
The storage device holds the operating system, applications, and personal files. This may be an HDD or SSD. Storage capacity and performance should be considered before formatting because formatting can erase existing data.

Processor:
The CPU is responsible for executing instructions and performing computations. Important processor specifications include the number of physical cores and logical processors/threads.

Physical Cores:
A processor core is an actual processing unit within the CPU. A processor with multiple cores can handle multiple tasks more effectively.

Logical Processors/Threads:
Logical processors represent the processing threads presented to the operating system. Technologies such as Intel Hyper-Threading can allow one physical core to appear as multiple logical processors.

ROM:
I also heard ROM mentioned when discussing system specifications. ROM traditionally refers to Read-Only Memory, although when discussing a computer's available capacity, people sometimes use "ROM" informally when they actually mean storage. For an operating system installation, the relevant consideration is normally the computer's storage capacity (HDD/SSD), rather than ROM in the strict technical sense.

8. GENERAL CONSIDERATIONS BEFORE OS INSTALLATION

Before formatting or installing an operating system, important factors include:

* RAM capacity.
* Storage type and available storage space.
* Processor model and generation.
* Number of physical CPU cores.
* Number of logical processors/threads.
* Whether the computer uses Legacy BIOS or UEFI.
* Whether the disk uses MBR or GPT.
* Compatibility between the selected boot mode and partitioning scheme.
* The requirements of the operating system being installed.
* Backing up important files before formatting.

9. OVERALL KNOWLEDGE GAINED

The day's activities introduced me to the relationship between computer hardware, firmware, disk partitioning, operating systems, bootable media, and virtualization.

I learned that creating a bootable USB is not simply a matter of copying an operating system file to a flash drive. The USB must be prepared with the appropriate boot files and configuration so that the computer's firmware can recognize it as a bootable device.

I also learned that Legacy BIOS and UEFI are different boot environments, while MBR and GPT are different disk partitioning systems. Rufus can be used to prepare bootable media while allowing the appropriate partition scheme and target boot system to be selected.

The introduction to Type 1 and Type 2 hypervisors also helped me understand how virtual machines can be run either directly on hardware or through an existing operating system.

Overall, Day 8 provided practical knowledge of bootable USB creation, Rufus, Legacy BIOS, UEFI, CSM, GPT, MBR, VMware virtualization, hypervisor types, processors, RAM, storage, CPU cores, and logical processors, as well as the factors that should be considered when preparing a computer for operating system installation or formatting.

# SIWES Day 9 — Comprehensive Experience and Technical Knowledge

## 1. General Activities

During Day 9 of my SIWES, I worked on **reports, system configuration, and documentation**. I configured a computer system and, through the process, gained an understanding of approximately **30% of the company's IT procedures and system-management practices**. I recorded important observations and technical knowledge in my notes for future reference.

The experience gave me a better understanding of what the company's IT department does to make computers accessible, manageable, secure, and connected to the organization's infrastructure.

## 2. Joining Computers to the Company Domain

I learned that company computers are introduced into the organization's **domain** using a wired network connection, specifically an **Ethernet/LAN cable**.

The domain allows the organization to centrally manage its computers and users. A domain environment can be managed using technologies such as **Microsoft Active Directory (AD)**.

Through a domain, IT administrators can manage:

* User accounts and passwords
* Computer accounts
* Security policies
* User permissions
* Software and system configurations
* Access to shared resources
* Organizational rules across multiple computers

This means that computers within the company do not operate completely independently; they can be centrally controlled and managed by the IT department.

## 3. VNC and Remote Computer Access

I observed that the IT department installs **VNC (Virtual Network Computing)** on computers so that IT personnel can remotely access and control them.

Remote-access software allows an IT technician to troubleshoot or configure a computer without necessarily being physically present at the user's workstation.

Possible uses include:

* Troubleshooting computer problems
* Installing or configuring software
* Changing system settings
* Assisting users remotely
* Performing maintenance
* Diagnosing problems

Other technologies that perform similar functions include **Windows Remote Desktop (RDP), AnyDesk, and TeamViewer**.

## 4. Centralized File Server

I learned that the company has a **centralized file server** that can be accessed through a particular IP address. The server contains applications and other resources used within the organization.

Instead of every computer independently obtaining applications from the internet, the organization can maintain software and resources centrally.

A file server can provide:

* Centralized storage
* Shared folders
* Application installers
* Company documents
* Network-accessible resources
* Controlled access through permissions

This gives the IT department greater control over what software and resources are available to users.

## 5. Network and Windows Update Management

I observed that the company restricts or blocks certain **Windows/system updates**.

The purpose is not necessarily that updates are unimportant, but that uncontrolled updates can create problems in a business environment.

Possible reasons for controlling updates include:

* Reducing network bandwidth consumption
* Preventing many computers from downloading large updates simultaneously
* Avoiding unexpected system restarts
* Preventing an update from disrupting normal operations
* Testing updates before deploying them across company computers
* Maintaining compatibility with business applications

Technologies such as **Group Policy** or **Windows Server Update Services (WSUS)** can be used in organizations to centrally control Windows updates.

## 6. Server Synchronization and Backup

I learned that company computers are synchronized with a server so that selected user data can be backed up.

The organization chooses which folders should be included in the backup. The commonly selected folders I observed were:

* **Documents**
* **Desktop**
* **Downloads**

These locations are important because users frequently save work-related files there.

The organization also determines which file types should **not** be backed up.

Examples I observed include:

* `.exe` — executable/application files
* `.iso` — disk-image files
* Music files
* Video files

The reasoning is that some of these files may be large, easily replaceable, unnecessary for business continuity, or better obtained from their original installation source rather than occupying backup storage.

## 7. PST Files

I noticed that **`.pst` files are included in the backup**, although I initially did not know what they were.

A `.pst` file is a **Microsoft Outlook Personal Storage Table** file.

It can contain Outlook information such as:

* Emails
* Email attachments
* Contacts
* Calendar information
* Other Outlook data

Because business email can contain important communications and records, backing up `.pst` files can be useful in an organization's data-protection strategy.

## 8. Administrator and Standard User Accounts

I also observed that IT personnel configure themselves as **Administrators**, while the actual computer users are assigned as **Other Users/standard users** rather than administrators.

An administrator account has significantly greater control over a computer. It can generally:

* Install software
* Change system settings
* Manage users
* Configure security settings
* Modify system-level configurations

A standard user has more restricted permissions.

This is an important cybersecurity principle known as **least privilege**: users should receive only the permissions they need to perform their jobs.

Restricting ordinary users can help reduce:

* Accidental system changes
* Unauthorized software installation
* Malware impact
* Configuration errors
* Unauthorized access to administrative functions

## 9. Windows Terminal/Command-Line Knowledge

During the experience, I also learned and observed the use of **Windows Terminal/Command Prompt commands** to make certain IT tasks easier.

Command-line tools can help IT personnel quickly:

* Retrieve system information
* Diagnose network problems
* Check IP configurations
* Test connectivity
* Identify computer information
* Perform administrative tasks

This showed me that IT support is not only about using graphical interfaces; command-line knowledge can make troubleshooting and system administration faster and more efficient.

## 10. What This Day Taught Me About Corporate IT

The main lesson from Day 9 was that company IT is much more than repairing individual computers.

The IT department creates an environment in which computers are:

**Connected → Managed → Remotely Accessible → Secured → Backed Up → Centrally Controlled**

I began seeing how several different IT areas work together:

* **Networking** provides connectivity.
* **Active Directory/domain services** provide centralized identity and computer management.
* **VNC/remote access** allows IT personnel to support systems remotely.
* **File servers** provide centralized resources.
* **Group Policy/update management** helps enforce organizational policies.
* **Backup systems** protect important user data.
* **User permissions** help maintain security.
* **Command-line tools** make administration and troubleshooting more efficient.

## SIWES Day 13 — Comprehensive Summary

### 1. Reports and Maintenance Tickets

* This exposed me to the relationship between **IT support, issue reporting, documentation, and maintenance activities**.
* The ticketing process provides a way to track reported faults and the actions taken to resolve them.

### 2. Accompanied Supervisors to Troubleshoot a Flap Barrier

* Accompanied my supervisors to investigate and repair a **flap barrier** that was giving electric shocks to people using it.
* Observed how a technical fault in an access-control device can create both an operational problem and a safety hazard.
* Learned that troubleshooting involves inspecting the system, identifying the source of the fault, repairing it, and then checking for other related faults.

### 3. Electrical Tester

* Learned about and observed the use of an electrical **tester**, which resembles a screwdriver.
* The tester can light up when it comes into contact with a point carrying electrical power.
* It is used to help determine whether electricity/current is present at a particular point.
* This demonstrated the importance of checking for electrical power before working on electrical components.
* The experience introduced me to basic electrical fault-detection and safety practices in addition to my IT-related activities.

### 4. Cause of the Electric Shock

* The flap barrier was shocking people because of a **breach in a wire**.
* The breach involved a cut/damaged section of the wire that exposed its metallic/conductive parts.
* Because the conductive part was exposed, it created an unsafe condition in which users could come into contact with electricity.
* The damaged wire was identified and repaired.
* This showed me how physical damage to cables can result in electrical leakage and become a safety hazard.

### 5. Additional Fault Discovered After the Initial Repair

* Fixing the exposed/damaged wire resolved the electric-shock problem, but another fault was discovered.
* A **resistor had burnt out**, and the **motherboard was also damaged**.
* These damaged electronic components affected the normal operation of the flap barrier system.
* This demonstrated that a single device can have multiple faults and that resolving the obvious problem does not necessarily mean the entire system is functioning correctly.

### 6. Biometric Device and Flap Barrier Communication

* The flap barrier was connected to a **biometric device** used as part of the access-control system.
* The biometric device was supposed to send commands/signals to the flap barrier.
* Because of the damaged resistor and motherboard, communication between the biometric device and the barrier was no longer functioning properly.
* The biometric device was no longer able to give the expected commands to the barrier, or the barrier was no longer receiving/responding to the signals from the biometric device.
* This helped me understand that access-control systems depend on communication between multiple hardware components rather than operating as isolated devices.

### 7. Technical Concepts Learned

The day's activities exposed me to several concepts worth studying further:

**Electrical fault**

* A problem involving unintended electrical behavior in a circuit or device.
* Can result from damaged wires, exposed conductors, failed components, short circuits, or other electrical problems.

**Exposed conductor**

* When the protective insulation around a wire is damaged or cut, exposing the conductive metal inside.
* Can create a shock hazard and potentially cause short circuits or equipment damage.

**Electrical tester**

* A basic tool used to detect the presence of electrical voltage/current at a point.
* The screwdriver-style tester observed today illuminates when electrical potential is detected.

**Resistor**

* An electronic component that controls/limits current in a circuit.
* A burnt resistor can indicate component failure or another underlying electrical problem.

**Motherboard**

* The main circuit board connecting and coordinating different electronic components of a device.
* Damage to it can interfere with communication and normal operation.

**Biometric access control**

* An access-control system that uses biometric information, such as fingerprints or facial characteristics, to identify/authenticate a person.
* The biometric system can communicate with a physical access-control device such as a flap barrier.

**Flap barrier**

* A physical access-control mechanism used to regulate movement through an entrance.
* It can receive authorization signals from systems such as biometric readers and open/allow passage when access is approved.

**Device communication**

* Electronic devices often need to exchange signals or commands to work together.
* If a circuit board or electronic component responsible for that communication is damaged, one device may stop responding to another even when the devices themselves appear physically intact.

### 8. Main Practical Lessons

* Technical support involves more than computers and networks; IT personnel may also work with **access-control, biometric, electrical, and electronic systems**.
* Physical inspection is an important part of troubleshooting.
* Electrical safety should be considered before handling equipment that may contain live power.
* A fault can have **multiple causes or stages**, so troubleshooting should continue after the first problem appears to be solved.
* Damaged cables can cause safety problems as well as equipment malfunction.
* Electronic components such as resistors and motherboards can affect the communication and operation of larger systems.
* Access-control systems depend on the interaction between several components: **biometric reader → electronic control system → barrier**.
* Proper reporting and ticketing are important for organizing technical maintenance and keeping records of faults and repairs.

DAY 14
Here is an extensive, structured summary of everything discussed for **SIWES Day 14**, including your observations, what was clarified, and the technical knowledge extracted from them.

## SIWES Day 14 — Detailed Knowledge Summary

### 1. Laptop Troubleshooting and RAM Generations

While Mr. Yousuf was checking a laptop that had issues, I heard and became familiar with the terms **DDR4** and **DDR5**.

DDR4 and DDR5 are different generations of **RAM (Random Access Memory)** used in computers. DDR5 is a newer generation that generally provides higher memory bandwidth and improved power efficiency compared with DDR4.

An important practical lesson is that RAM generations are not generally interchangeable. A laptop designed for DDR4 requires compatible DDR4 memory, while a DDR5-compatible system requires DDR5 memory. The physical and electrical specifications of the motherboard determine which type of RAM can be installed.

### 2. Avaya Device Installation and VoIP

I had the opportunity to install and troubleshoot an **Avaya communication device**. The device had stopped working because it was no longer receiving power through the Ethernet cable.

The issue was related to **Power over Ethernet (PoE)**. PoE allows both network communication and electrical power to be delivered to a compatible device through the same Ethernet cable.

Since the Ethernet connection was no longer providing power to the Avaya device, it was continuously powered using an external **charger/power adapter**, which allowed the device to operate again.

I also learned that Avaya devices can be used for **VoIP (Voice over Internet Protocol)** communication. VoIP is a technology that allows voice calls to be transmitted over an IP/network infrastructure rather than relying exclusively on traditional telephone systems.

This observation helped me understand the relationship between:

* Ethernet networking
* PoE
* IP networks
* VoIP
* IP/office telephone systems
* Avaya communication equipment

A useful distinction is that **VoIP describes how the voice communication is carried over the network**, while **PoE describes how compatible network devices can receive electrical power through Ethernet**. They can therefore work together in an office telephone system.

### 3. Laptop Disassembly and Hardware Inspection

I observed the opening/disassembly of a laptop and was taught that there is no single procedure for opening every laptop.

Different laptop manufacturers and models have different internal designs. Some laptops are relatively straightforward to open, while others require more careful disassembly.

I observed that laptops may have:

* Easily accessible screws
* Hidden screws
* Screws located underneath rubber feet or covers
* Plastic clips holding the casing together
* Detachable/removable batteries
* Internal batteries that must be disconnected during servicing
* Different internal component layouts

The main lesson was that a technician should not assume that one laptop can be opened in exactly the same way as another. The specific model should be examined first, and unnecessary force should be avoided to prevent damage to the casing, clips, cables, or internal components.

### 4. Laptop Storage and SSD Upgrade Limitations

I also learned that not every laptop has room for an additional SSD.

Some laptop models may have:

* Only one storage slot
* A single M.2 slot
* No additional M.2 slot
* A motherboard design that does not provide another connector for storage
* Storage soldered directly to the motherboard in some designs

Therefore, before purchasing an additional SSD for a laptop, it is important to determine whether the specific laptop model actually supports an additional drive.

This introduced me to an important practical aspect of computer hardware maintenance: **upgradeability depends on the laptop's motherboard and physical design**, not simply on whether an SSD exists that could theoretically be installed.

### 5. STP Terminology Clarification

I also asked about the possibility of "switch core with default STP." The relevant networking term is likely **STP (Spanning Tree Protocol)**.

STP is a network protocol used on switches to help prevent **Layer 2 network loops** when there are redundant connections between switches.

The basic idea is that if multiple paths exist between switches, STP can place some redundant paths into a blocking state so that a loop does not continuously circulate network traffic.

Modern managed switches may use variants such as **RSTP (Rapid Spanning Tree Protocol)**. Therefore, "default STP" should not automatically be assumed to mean that every modern switch is using the original 802.1D STP; the actual default depends on the switch manufacturer and configuration.

### 6. Overall Knowledge Gained on Day 14

Day 14 exposed me to several areas of practical IT support and infrastructure:

**Computer hardware**

* DDR4 and DDR5 RAM
* Laptop internal components
* Laptop disassembly
* Battery types
* SSD expansion
* Hardware compatibility
* Laptop upgrade limitations

**Networking**

* Ethernet power delivery
* Power over Ethernet (PoE)
* Network-connected devices
* STP and prevention of network loops

**Communication systems**

* Avaya devices
* VoIP
* IP-based telephone systems
* Relationship between network connectivity and telephone operation

### 7. Practical Lessons

The major practical lessons from the day were:

1. **Hardware specifications matter:** RAM types such as DDR4 and DDR5 are different generations and require compatible hardware.
2. **Network cables can carry more than data:** With PoE, Ethernet can provide both network connectivity and electrical power.
3. **VoIP depends on network infrastructure:** IP-based phones communicate through a network rather than operating like traditional standalone telephone systems.
4. **Troubleshooting should identify the actual failure point:** In the Avaya case, the problem was not simply that the device had stopped working; the power supplied through Ethernet was no longer available.
5. **Laptop servicing is model-specific:** Different laptops require different disassembly procedures.
6. **Not every laptop is upgradeable:** The motherboard and physical design determine whether additional RAM or SSD storage can be installed.
7. **Networking redundancy requires control:** STP helps prevent loops when switches have multiple interconnected paths.

### 8. Possible Technical Topics to Study Further

Based on Day 14, useful topics to study next include:

* DDR4 vs DDR5 RAM
* RAM compatibility and laptop RAM specifications
* M.2 SSDs and SATA SSDs
* NVMe vs SATA
* Laptop motherboard layouts
* Laptop disassembly and hardware servicing
* Power over Ethernet (PoE)
* PoE standards such as 802.3af, 802.3at and 802.3bt
* VoIP fundamentals
* IP phones
* Avaya phone/network architecture
* Ethernet switching
* STP
* RSTP
* Root bridge and root port
* Layer 2 network loops
* Basic network troubleshooting

### 9. Concise Logbook Version

I also participated in the installation and troubleshooting of an Avaya VoIP device that had stopped receiving power through Ethernet. The issue was resolved by supplying external power using a charger, introducing me to the practical relationship between VoIP devices and Power over Ethernet (PoE). I further observed the disassembly of a laptop and learned that different laptop models have different opening procedures, including hidden screws and different battery designs. I also learned that some laptops do not have provisions for additional SSD storage because of their motherboard design.

### What I interpreted and added

I converted your description into a more formal SIWES activity entry. I explained that:

* You practiced **crimping/terminating LAN cables**.
* The **RJ45 connector** is the connector commonly attached to Ethernet/LAN cables.
* A **stripping tool** is used to remove the outer insulation/sheath of a cable and expose the internal conductors.
* A **crimping tool** is used to secure the RJ45 connector onto the cable after the wires have been arranged correctly.
* **Cat 6 UTP** refers to **Category 6 Unshielded Twisted Pair** Ethernet cable.
* I mentioned that the internal wires of the Cat 6 cable need to be **properly arranged before termination**.
* Regarding the metal connector you saw, I suggested that it may have been a **metal-shielded RJ45 connector**, rather than a standard plastic RJ45 connector.
* I explained that shielded connectors can provide additional protection against **electromagnetic/electrical interference**, particularly when used with compatible shielded cabling.

### Key technical concepts from Day 15

| Term                         | Meaning / Relevance                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| ---------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **LAN cable**                | A network cable used to connect devices to a Local Area Network.                                                                                                                                                          |
| **Crimping**                 | The process of attaching and securing a connector, such as an RJ45 plug, to the end of an Ethernet cable.                                                                                                                 |
| **RJ45**                     | Commonly used name for the modular 8-contact plug used for Ethernet cables.                                                                                                                                               |
| **Stripping tool**           | Tool used to remove the outer jacket/insulation of a network cable without damaging the internal conductors.                                                                                                              |
| **Crimping tool**            | Tool used to terminate and secure the Ethernet connector onto the cable.                                                                                                                                                  |
| **Cat 6 UTP**                | Category 6 Unshielded Twisted Pair Ethernet cable. It contains twisted pairs of copper conductors and is widely used for network connections.                                                                             |
| **Metal/shielded connector** | The metal connector you observed may have been a shielded Ethernet/RJ45-type plug. Its metal shielding is intended to help reduce electromagnetic interference when used as part of a compatible shielded cabling system. |

### Skills gained

Day 15 involved practical exposure to **Ethernet cable termination**. The main skills/knowledge gained were:

1. Identifying a **Cat 6 UTP cable**.
2. Identifying an **RJ45-type Ethernet connector**.
3. Using a **cable stripping tool**.
4. Using a **crimping tool**.
5. Understanding the basic process of preparing a LAN cable for termination.
6. Becoming more familiar with common **networking hardware terminology**.
7. Observing different types of Ethernet connectors, including a possible **shielded/metal connector**.

### Suitable SIWES record

DAY 19
Below is a consolidated, detailed record of **everything discussed for SIWES Day 19**, keeping both your original observations and the explanations added during our discussion.

## SIWES Day 19 — Detailed Experience Record

### 1. PowerShell and Terminal Commands

During Day 19 of my SIWES, I learned about commands used in **Windows Terminal and PowerShell**. I learned that PowerShell commands can be broadly encountered in three forms:

* **Cmdlets** — built-in PowerShell commands that perform specific administrative or system tasks.
* **Functions** — commands based on reusable blocks of PowerShell code, which can be created by users or provided by scripts/modules.
* **Aliases** — alternative, usually shorter names for commands. They make it possible to execute certain commands using shorter names.

I learned that the `Get-Command` command can be used to examine available commands and identify their command type. For example, `Get-Command` can show whether a particular command is a cmdlet, function, alias, or another command type.

This introduced me to **PowerShell as a system-administration and troubleshooting tool**, rather than simply using the graphical Windows interface.

### 2. Retrieving a Computer's BIOS Serial Number

I learned how to retrieve a computer's **BIOS/system serial number** using PowerShell.

The older command I was familiar with was:

`wmic bios get serialnumber`

However, WMIC may not be available on some newer Windows installations. I learned an alternative PowerShell method:

`Get-WmiObject Win32_BIOS`

The `Win32_BIOS` class provides information about the computer's BIOS, including its serial number. This taught me an alternative method of obtaining hardware identification information when the older WMIC command does not work.

### 3. Synology Configuration

I worked on **Synology**, a network-attached storage (NAS) system used for centralized storage and management of files and data within an organization.

I learned/assisted with configuring the Synology system. In an organizational environment, Synology NAS can provide centralized storage, file access, backup capabilities, and other network storage functions.

This gave me practical exposure to **network storage and infrastructure administration**.

### 4. Group Policy

I also worked with **Group Policy** and updated Group Policy settings.

Group Policy is a Windows administration feature used to centrally control and configure settings for users and computers within a network/domain environment.

It can be used to manage things such as:

* Security settings
* User and computer configurations
* System restrictions
* Administrative policies
* Organizational IT standards

Updating Group Policy exposed me to the way IT administrators can apply or refresh configurations across computers rather than configuring every computer individually.

### 5. Monitoring Encryption

I monitored the **encryption status** of systems.

Encryption is a security mechanism that protects information by transforming readable data into a form that cannot be easily understood without the appropriate key or authorization.

Monitoring encryption status is important in an organization because it helps IT personnel confirm that required security protections are active and that systems or drives are properly protected.

This connected my practical SIWES experience to **cybersecurity and information protection**, which was particularly relevant because I had also started learning about cybersecurity during my SIWES.

### 6. Server Room Status Documentation

I recorded the **status of the server room**.

The server room contains important IT infrastructure and therefore needs to be monitored and documented. Recording its status can help with:

* Tracking the condition of equipment
* Identifying potential problems
* Maintaining records
* Monitoring the state of the infrastructure
* Supporting preventive maintenance

This gave me experience with **IT documentation and infrastructure monitoring**, rather than only hands-on technical work.

### 7. Monitor Replacement

I followed **Tumise, another IT student**, while he worked on replacing a faulty monitor.

The monitor had an issue described as an **ink/display problem**. I observed the process of replacing the affected monitor.

This provided additional exposure to **computer hardware troubleshooting and maintenance**, particularly the practical process of identifying a faulty peripheral and replacing it.

---

## Overall Learning from Day 19

Day 19 exposed me to several areas of IT administration and maintenance:

1. **PowerShell and command-line administration** — learning about cmdlets, functions, aliases, and `Get-Command`.
2. **Hardware/system identification** — retrieving BIOS serial numbers using PowerShell.
3. **Network storage** — configuring Synology NAS.
4. **Windows administration** — updating Group Policy.
5. **Cybersecurity** — monitoring encryption status.

8. **Technical documentation** — recording infrastructure and equipment information.

Overall, the day helped me understand how IT support involves a combination of **system administration, networking, cybersecurity, hardware maintenance, monitoring, troubleshooting, and documentation**. It also strengthened my understanding of using command-line tools for tasks that may not always be convenient or possible through the graphical user interface.

# SIWES Day 20 — Detailed Knowledge Summary

## 1. Microsoft Teams Configuration

### What I did

* I configured Microsoft Teams.
* This involved setting up Microsoft Teams so that it could be used properly for communication and collaboration.

### What “configure” means

To **configure** a software, computer, device, or system means to **set it up, adjust its settings, or customize it so that it works correctly for a particular user or purpose**.

For Microsoft Teams, configuration can include activities such as:

* Opening or installing the application.
* Signing a user into their account.
* Setting up user preferences.
* Configuring audio and video settings.
* Selecting the appropriate microphone, speakers, or camera.
* Checking permissions.
* Ensuring that the application can be used properly for meetings, messaging, and collaboration.

### General meaning of configuration

Configuration is essentially **preparing a system to function according to its intended requirements**.

Examples:

* **Configuring Microsoft Teams:** setting it up for communication and collaboration.
* **Configuring a laptop:** adjusting system settings, installing necessary software, connecting networks, changing preferences, or preparing the device for a user.
* **Configuring a network device:** setting IP addresses, network parameters, ports, VLANs, or other settings required for the device to communicate properly.

Therefore, saying **“I configured Microsoft Teams”** means that I performed the necessary setup or adjustments to make Microsoft Teams functional and suitable for the user's needs.

* Opening Windows Settings.

* Applying the changes.

Assisting with a biometric issue means helping investigate why the biometric system was not functioning correctly and taking steps to restore normal operation.

**Configure:** To set up, adjust, or customize a system so that it works correctly for a particular purpose.

**Troubleshoot:** To systematically investigate a problem, identify its cause, and apply a solution.

**Biometric system:** A system that uses measurable biological characteristics, such as fingerprints, to identify or authenticate people.

**System settings:** Options within an operating system that control how the computer behaves or appears, such as language, display, network, accounts, and other preferences.

## SIWES Day 22 — Comprehensive Summary

### 1. Job Search and Career Advice from Mr. Felix

Mr. Felix discussed important things to consider when looking for a job. The areas he highlighted included:

* **Scouting for job opportunities** — actively looking for suitable job openings rather than waiting for opportunities to come.
* **Applying for jobs** — submitting applications for positions that match your qualifications and interests.
* **Building an attractive CV** — creating a professional CV that presents your qualifications, skills, and experience effectively.
* **Preparing for and facing interviews** — understanding that getting an interview is an important stage of the job-search process and requires good preparation and communication.

### 2. Important Things to Consider When Building a CV

The discussion included several points about creating a good CV:

* A CV should follow **current professional CV standards and trends** rather than using outdated formats.
* The CV should be **tailored to the specific job** being applied for. This means emphasizing qualifications, experience, and skills that are relevant to the requirements of the particular position.
* **Hobbies and interests should be appropriate** and should not contradict the nature, expectations, or professional image of the job being applied for.
* The CV should include relevant **soft skills**, such as:

  * Teamwork
  * Communication
  * Customer relations
  * Other interpersonal and workplace skills relevant to the position

The main lesson was that a CV should not simply be a general list of everything a person has done. It should present the applicant in a way that is relevant to the particular job.

### 3. Importance of the First Phone Call

Mr. Felix also emphasized that the first phone call you receive concerning a job or interview can be very important.

Important lessons included:

* One should communicate **respectfully and professionally** when answering calls.
* You should remain respectful even when you do not know who is calling.
* The way you communicate on the first call can create an impression of your professionalism.
* **Communication skills are important** during the entire job-search process, not only during the formal interview.

This means professionalism begins before the actual interview. The way an applicant communicates through phone calls, messages, applications, and other interactions can influence how they are perceived.

### 4. Application Writing

You also learnt that you should develop the ability to **write a good job application/application letter**.

This was identified as another important part of the job-search process, alongside preparing a CV and attending interviews.

### 5. Practical Work Done

Apart from the career discussion:

* You **wrote reports** as part of your SIWES activities.
* You followed **Tumise** while connecting an Ethernet cable to an **Avaya device/phone**.
* During the process, you learnt several networking concepts involving Ethernet ports, network switches, and Power over Ethernet (POE).

### 6. What You Learnt About Ethernet Ports and Switches

You discovered that Ethernet ports on network switches can be **disabled or turned off through switch configuration**.

This means that even when an Ethernet cable and connected device appear physically fine, the connection may not work if the corresponding switch port has been administratively disabled.

### 7. POE Issues on Switches

You also learnt that some network switches can have **POE (Power over Ethernet) problems**.

POE allows certain network devices to receive electrical power through their Ethernet connection instead of requiring a separate power adapter.

An Avaya IP phone is an example of a device that can use POE.

### 8. Why an Avaya Phone May Not Work With a Faulty POE Port

Because the Avaya device is a POE-powered device, it depends on the switch being able to provide power through the Ethernet port.

Therefore:

* If the Ethernet port is connected to a switch that has a POE fault,
* and the Avaya phone is relying on POE for power,
* the Avaya phone may fail to power on or operate correctly.

The important distinction is that the Ethernet connection may potentially still provide network connectivity while the **power-delivery portion of the connection is faulty**, depending on the exact switch fault and configuration.

### 9. Connecting a Desktop Through an Avaya Phone

You also learnt that a desktop computer can receive network/internet access through an Avaya IP phone.

A common arrangement is:

**Network switch → Avaya phone → Desktop computer**

The Avaya phone has Ethernet ports that can allow the computer to connect to the network through the phone.

### 10. Why a Desktop Can Still Work When POE Has a Problem

You correctly observed that a desktop computer does not require POE.

The desktop normally receives its electrical power from its own power supply and only uses the Ethernet cable for network connectivity.

Therefore, if a switch has a **POE-specific problem**, a desktop connected to a functioning data connection may still be able to access the network even though a POE-dependent device such as an Avaya phone cannot power itself.

The important distinction is:

* **POE:** provides electrical power through Ethernet.
* **Ethernet data:** provides network communication.
* A device can require POE for power while another device only requires Ethernet for data.

### 11. Overall Lessons From Day 22

Day 22 combined **career development** with **practical networking experience**.

The career-related lessons were:

* Scout for suitable job opportunities.
* Apply for relevant positions.
* Keep your CV current and professional.
* Tailor your CV to each job.
* Present relevant skills and qualifications.
* Ensure hobbies/interests do not create an inappropriate impression for the role.
* Develop teamwork, communication, and customer-relations skills.
* Communicate respectfully and professionally during phone calls.
* Understand that the first interaction with a potential employer matters.
* Learn to write effective job applications.
* Prepare properly for interviews.

The technical lessons were:

* Ethernet switch ports can be administratively disabled.
* Network switches can experience POE faults.
* Avaya IP phones can depend on POE for power.
* A POE fault can prevent a POE-dependent device from functioning.
* A desktop can be connected to the network through an Avaya phone.
* Desktop computers do not require POE because they have their own power source.
* POE and Ethernet data connectivity are related but distinct functions.

### 12. Accuracy of Your Original Understanding

Your original observations were **mostly correct**. The main issues were not with the concepts but with grammar, spelling, and the way some technical points were expressed.

In particular, your understanding that:

* an Ethernet switch port can be turned off,
* switches can have POE problems,
* Avaya phones can depend on POE,
* a desktop can receive network access through an Avaya phone, and
* desktops do not require POE

was fundamentally correct.

The technical explanation simply needed clearer wording and the distinction between **POE power delivery** and **Ethernet data connectivity** to be made more precise.
