DAY 24
Here is an extensive extraction-ready summary of **everything you said and everything I added/corrected for SIWES Day 24**:

## SIWES Day 24 — Detailed Summary

### 1. Ethernet Socket Testing / LAN Cable Testing

* I worked with **Mr. Yousuf** to test Ethernet network sockets.
* I used a **smaller/slimmer LAN cable tester** connected to an Ethernet wall socket or network outlet.
* Mr. Yousuf worked from the **switch side**, using a larger LAN tester/device.
* The Ethernet socket I was testing was connected through the network cabling to a particular port on the network switch.
* When Mr. Yousuf inserted the tester/cable into the corresponding switch port, the LED indicators on my LAN tester lit up.
* When he removed the connection from the switch port, the lights on my LAN tester went off.
* This demonstrated that there was a physical connection/continuity between the switch port and the Ethernet socket I was testing.
* The test helped verify that the Ethernet cabling and termination between the network switch and the wall socket were functioning.
* In professional terms, this can be described as **testing Ethernet outlet connectivity and cable continuity using LAN cable testers**.
* The basic principle was that the two testers were effectively checking whether the cable path between the switch and the network outlet was complete and properly connected.

### 2. Remote Printer Installation

* I remotely installed a printer on a user's laptop.
* The installation was carried out without physically being beside the user's computer.
* This involved remotely accessing/configuring the laptop and setting up the printer so that the user could use it.
* This can be described professionally as **remote printer installation and configuration**.
* Depending on the exact process used, this could involve installing the printer driver, adding the printer, configuring its connection, and making it available to the user.

### 3. Report Writing / Documentation

* I also worked on **writing reports** during the day.
* This involved documenting IT-related activities and work carried out.
* This demonstrates exposure to **technical documentation and IT reporting**, rather than only practical hardware/network activities.

### 4. Observation of HPE Aruba Switch Configuration

* I observed the configuration of an **HPE Aruba 6000-series switch**.
* The exact model/name was uncertain at the time, but it was identified as an **HPE Aruba 6000** with a model/reference that sounded like **R898A/R8xxA**.
* I learned that network switches can differ in how their initial management configuration is performed.
* Some switch models may have a **default management IP address**, which can allow an administrator to access the switch over the network and configure it.
* The particular HPE Aruba switch being configured did **not have a usable default management IP configured for the required setup**.
* Because of this, it had to be accessed directly through its **console interface** for initial configuration.

### 5. Console Configuration Using USB Type-C

* The switch was accessed using a **USB Type-C console connection**.
* A Type-C cable was connected from the computer to the switch's console interface.
* A terminal/console application, **PuTTY**, was used on the computer to communicate with the switch.
* Instead of using a graphical interface, the configuration was performed through the **command-line interface (CLI)**.
* Commands were entered through the terminal to configure the switch.
* This allowed the administrator to interact directly with the switch's operating system and configuration environment.

### 6. Assigning a Management IP Address

* One of the things I learned was that the switch initially needed a management IP address to allow easier network-based management.
* The management configuration involved the switch's **default VLAN 1 interface**.
* A management IP address was assigned to **VLAN 1** through the command line.
* This is different from simply assigning an IP address to a physical switch port. The IP is associated with the VLAN interface, commonly referred to as an **SVI (Switched Virtual Interface)**.
* VLAN 1 is commonly present by default on many switches, although the exact configuration and recommended management practices depend on the switch model and network design.
* After configuring a management IP, the switch can potentially be managed over the network rather than requiring a direct console connection every time.

### 7. What I Learned Technically

The main technical concepts I was exposed to were:

* Ethernet wall socket testing
* LAN cable testers
* Ethernet cable continuity

* HPE Aruba switches
* HPE Aruba 6000-series equipment

* VLAN 1
* VLAN interfaces/SVIs
* Network device management

### 8. Important Clarification About the Switch

My initial understanding was that the switch "had no IP." More precisely, the important point is that **there was no usable/default management IP configured for the required initial access**. A switch can operate at Layer 2 without having a management IP at all; the management IP is primarily used for accessing and managing the device over the network.

The switch therefore had to be accessed locally through its **console port** before a management IP could be configured.

### 9. Overall Description of Day 24

Day 24 exposed me to both **physical network troubleshooting and network-device configuration**. I tested Ethernet sockets from the switch side and outlet side using LAN testers, which helped me understand how physical network connectivity can be verified. I also remotely installed a printer on a user's laptop and worked on technical reports. In addition, I observed the initial configuration of an HPE Aruba 6000-series switch, learning how a switch without an immediately usable management IP can be accessed through a USB Type-C console connection using PuTTY. I observed how command-line instructions were used to configure the switch and assign a management IP to its VLAN 1 interface. The switch configuration initially appeared complex because it was performed entirely through CLI commands rather than a graphical interface.

## Networking

### Ping

The `ping` command can be used to test whether a device or network destination is reachable.

It can be used to:

* Test connectivity between devices.
* Check whether a server is reachable.
* Troubleshoot network problems.
* Observe response times.
* Detect intermittent connectivity problems.

### `ping -t`

The `-t` option causes Windows to continue sending ping requests continuously until the user stops the command.

This makes it useful for monitoring network stability over time rather than performing only a limited number of ping requests.

# 6. Structured Cabling

The punch-down tool introduced another part of structured network cabling.

A punch-down tool is commonly used to terminate Ethernet cables into:

* Keystone jacks.
* Wall-mounted network face plates.
* Patch panels.

The individual wires of an Ethernet cable are inserted into designated slots and secured using the tool.

The face plate provides the physical network outlet that users can connect an Ethernet cable to.

This connects the physical cabling infrastructure to the network equipment, such as switches.

DAY 28
Below is a comprehensive extraction of **everything discussed for SIWES Day 28**, including your original observations and the technical clarifications added afterward.

## SIWES Day 28 — Comprehensive Summary

### 1. Continuation of Previous Networking Tasks

* Day 28 included the **first four tasks from SIWES Day 26** as part of the day's activities.
* These tasks were related to network infrastructure, connectivity testing, and fibre/network maintenance.

### 2. Learning About Fibre Optic Cables

* I learned more about **fibre optic cables** and how they are installed and connected.
* I initially compared the process with Ethernet cable crimping but learned that fibre optic cables are not normally terminated by crimping in the same way.
* The process used to join fibre optic cables is called **splicing**.
* Fibre optic splicing is a specialized task generally performed by trained professionals because it requires precision and specialized equipment.
* I was introduced to the idea that fibre optic cables transmit information using **light**, unlike traditional copper Ethernet cables, which transmit electrical signals.
* Technical terms related to fibre optics were mentioned, although I was unfamiliar with some of them and would need further research to fully understand them.
* Two major types of fibre splicing that were identified for further study are:

  * **Fusion splicing** — joining fibre strands using heat.
  * **Mechanical splicing** — aligning and joining fibres mechanically without fusion.

### 3. Fibre and LAN Patch Cables

* I learned about **fibre optic patch cables** and **LAN/Ethernet patch cables**.
* A patch cable is generally a relatively short cable used to connect network equipment, ports, patch panels, or other components within a network.
* A **fibre optic patch cable** is used for fibre connections.
* A **LAN patch cable** is an Ethernet/copper cable used for network connections.
* I also encountered the concept of **patch ports**, which are used as connection points within structured network cabling systems.
* These components help organize network connections and make troubleshooting, maintenance, and changes to network infrastructure easier.

### 4. Ethernet Cable Crimping

* I successfully **crimped an Ethernet cable**.
* The practical exercise helped me understand Ethernet cable termination better rather than simply performing the process mechanically.
* I learned more about the individual twisted-pair wires inside an Ethernet cable and how they relate to network communication.
* The cable contains four twisted pairs:

  * Orange pair
  * Green pair
  * Blue pair
  * Brown pair
* Based on what I observed and was taught, the **orange and green pairs** are used for data transmission in basic two-pair Ethernet connections.
* The blue and brown pairs may remain unused in certain basic non-PoE Ethernet implementations.
* I learned that **standard non-PoE Ethernet** can operate using two pairs/four conductors under the relevant Ethernet standards.
* I also learned that **PoE (Power over Ethernet)** can use all four pairs/eight conductors to transmit both network data and electrical power.
* This helped me understand why the wiring requirements can differ depending on whether a network connection is carrying only data or both data and power.

### 5. Important Technical Clarification About Ethernet Pairs

* The practical observation about orange/green versus blue/brown should not be interpreted as meaning that blue and brown are inherently "power cables."
* All four twisted pairs are part of the Ethernet cable.
* In certain older/two-pair Ethernet configurations, only two pairs are required for data, while PoE implementations can use the additional pairs depending on the PoE standard.
* The exact pins used depend on the Ethernet wiring standard and PoE implementation.
* This distinction is useful when documenting the practical lesson accurately.

### 6. Printer Configuration and Installation

* I **configured and installed a printer fully**.
* This was a practical exercise involving setting up a printer so that it could be used properly.
* The task strengthened my experience with printer installation and configuration.
* Depending on the particular printer and environment, a complete installation can involve connecting the printer, installing the appropriate driver/software, configuring network or device settings, and testing that the printer works correctly.
* This activity added to my practical IT support and device-configuration experience.

### 7. Main Skills and Knowledge Gained

Day 28 exposed me to several areas of practical IT and networking:

* Fibre optic cable fundamentals
* Fibre optic splicing
* Difference between fibre and copper network cabling
* Fibre optic patch cables
* LAN/Ethernet patch cables
* Patch ports and structured cabling
* Ethernet cable crimping
* Understanding twisted pairs in Ethernet cables
* Basic distinction between non-PoE and PoE cabling
* Printer installation
* Printer configuration
* Practical network infrastructure maintenance
* General IT troubleshooting and equipment setup

### 8. Overall Learning

The day expanded my practical understanding beyond basic Ethernet cabling. I learned that different network media require different installation and termination techniques. I gained hands-on experience crimping Ethernet cables while also being introduced to professional fibre optic installation and splicing. I also gained experience with network-related printer configuration, giving me broader exposure to both networking infrastructure and general IT support.

### 3. Important Lessons from the Day

1. **Always save important work manually when auto-save is not guaranteed.**

   * Assuming that a program automatically saves can lead to lost work.
   * A safer practice is to save periodically and confirm that the file has actually been saved.

2. **Data entry requires accuracy and consistency.**

   * Entering a large amount of information requires concentration because small mistakes can affect records and reports.

3. **IT support includes office technology.**

   * IT work is not limited to networking, programming, or troubleshooting computers.
   * Supporting photocopiers, printers, document systems, and other office equipment can also form part of an IT support role.

4. **Preventive maintenance is important.**

   * Regular maintenance of desktops and laptops can help keep systems reliable and identify potential problems before they become major failures.

5. **Practical experience builds independence.**

   * Independently photocopying for the first time was a small but useful step toward being able to handle workplace tasks without constant supervision.

DAY 30
Here is a detailed, self-contained summary of **everything from SIWES Day 30**, including your original observations and the additional interpretation/explanations:

### SIWES Day 30 – Detailed Summary

On Day 30 of my SIWES training, I participated in the **maintenance of computer systems**, focusing on both software/system maintenance and physical hardware maintenance.

### 1. Windows System Cleanup and Maintenance

I participated in routine maintenance of computer systems by removing unnecessary and temporary files.

* I used **Windows + R (Run)** to access the `temp` folder and delete temporary files stored there.
* I also accessed the `%temp%` folder and deleted the temporary files stored there.
* I emptied the **Recycle Bin** after deleting unnecessary files.
* I used **Disk Cleanup (`cleanmgr`)** to identify and remove additional unnecessary files from the computer.
* These activities helped me understand that Windows accumulates temporary and unnecessary files over time and that periodically removing them can help maintain available storage space and general system cleanliness.

### 2. Power Options / Fast Startup

I opened what I believe was **Power Options using `powercfg.cpl`** and checked the **Fast Startup** setting.

* Fast Startup was enabled initially.
* I unchecked/disabled it as part of the maintenance procedure.
* I learned that Windows has power-management and startup settings that can be adjusted during troubleshooting and system maintenance.
* I also became more familiar with accessing Windows configuration settings through Run commands.

### 3. Group Policy Update

I also participated in updating **Group Policy**.

* This exposed me to another aspect of Windows system administration.
* Group Policy allows system settings and administrative policies to be applied and refreshed on Windows computers.
* Updating the policy helped me understand that maintenance can involve not only cleaning files but also ensuring that configuration policies are properly applied.

### 4. Synology Maintenance and Reconfiguration

I checked the **Synology system** to make sure it was functioning properly.

* I monitored the Synology systems to confirm that they were working correctly.
* When I noticed that one was not functioning as expected, I reconfigured it.
* This gave me practical exposure to basic troubleshooting and configuration of Synology systems/NAS equipment.
* I learned that part of IT maintenance involves checking whether network/storage systems are operational and taking corrective action when something is wrong.

### 5. Physical Maintenance of the System Unit

I also participated in physically cleaning computer systems.

* We opened/took apart the **system unit** for maintenance.
* Before using the blower, we removed the **RAM**.
* I learned how to properly remove a RAM module from a computer and how to reinsert it.
* The system unit was then cleaned using a **blower** to remove dust and accumulated debris from inside the computer.
* This gave me practical experience handling internal computer components rather than only working with software.
* I learned that components such as RAM should be handled carefully during hardware maintenance and should be removed when necessary before using compressed air/blowing equipment.

### 6. Cleaning External Computer Components

We also cleaned external computer equipment.

* The **monitor** was cleaned.
* The **keyboard** was also cleaned.
* A foam-like cleaning substance/material was used for cleaning the external surfaces.
* This showed me that preventive maintenance includes both internal components and external peripherals.

### Overall Experience and Skills Learned

Day 30 gave me practical experience in **preventive computer maintenance**, covering both software and hardware.

The software maintenance activities included:

* Accessing temporary files through Windows Run.
* Using `temp` and `%temp%`.
* Emptying the Recycle Bin.
* Using `cleanmgr`/Disk Cleanup.
* Checking and changing Fast Startup settings through Power Options.
* Updating Group Policy.
* Checking and reconfiguring Synology systems.

The hardware maintenance activities included:

* Opening and cleaning system units.
* Removing and reinstalling RAM.
* Using a blower to remove dust from internal computer components.
* Cleaning monitors and keyboards.

### Skills Developed

Through these activities, I gained practical exposure to:

* **Preventive computer maintenance**

The day was particularly useful because it combined **software maintenance, system administration, storage cleanup, troubleshooting, and physical hardware maintenance** rather than focusing on only one area of IT.



DAY 31
Below is a consolidated, detailed record of everything discussed for **SIWES Day 31**, including your observations, what you actually did, and the technical concepts explained.

# SIWES Day 31 — Comprehensive Activity and Learning Summary

## 1. Conference Room Maintenance

I participated in the maintenance and operational checking of a conference room system. The maintenance involved checking whether the different components of the conference room technology were functioning properly and could communicate with one another.

### HikVision Large Display/System

The conference room contained a large HikVision TV/display that was apparently part of an integrated conference-room system.

The large display and a laptop were configured with the same user information/profile, but they could also operate independently. This meant that the laptop and large display were not completely dependent on each other and could function separately when necessary.

The setup allowed content from the laptop to be displayed on the large screen during meetings and presentations.

### WonderCast

I learnt that WonderCast software was being used for wireless casting/screen mirroring between the laptop and the large display.

This allows a user to send the laptop's screen/content to the larger display without necessarily relying on a physical display cable.

The purpose is to make presentations, meetings, demonstrations, and shared viewing easier.

---

## 2. Yealink Conference Camera

The conference room also had a high-quality camera that I identified as a Yealink camera.

It appeared to be a PTZ-type conference camera because it could rotate in different directions and provide a wide range of movement.

I observed that:

* The camera could rotate in multiple directions.
* It produced very clear video.
* The image remained detailed even when zoomed in.
* It was connected to the laptop.
* The camera could be viewed through the laptop.
* Its video feed could also be displayed on the large conference-room screen.

This creates a complete conference-room video setup where people in the room can see the camera feed on the large display.

The camera also required cleaning and maintenance to preserve image quality, especially when using zoom.

### PTZ Meaning

PTZ means:

* **Pan** — horizontal movement, such as left and right.
* **Tilt** — vertical movement, such as up and down.
* **Zoom** — changing the camera's field of view to make distant objects appear closer.

PTZ cameras are useful when a single camera needs to cover a large area or when the operator needs to change the camera's viewing direction.

---

# 3. Visit to Another Branch

I also visited another branch of the company located across the road.

The branch was being prepared for a CCTV camera installation project.

The purpose of the visit was to observe the preparations being made before the cameras were actually installed.

---

## 4. CCTV Camera Types Observed

I saw three different types of CCTV cameras at the branch:

### Mini PTZ Camera

The Mini PTZ camera is a smaller PTZ-style surveillance camera capable of changing its viewing direction and, depending on the model, zooming into areas of interest.

It is useful where wider coverage and remote adjustment of the viewing direction are required.

### Bullet Camera

The Bullet camera has a long, cylindrical appearance.

It is commonly used for directional surveillance, particularly outdoors and around areas such as:

* Gates
* Perimeters
* Walkways
* Parking areas
* Building entrances

Its shape makes it suitable for directing the camera toward a particular area.

### Dome Camera

The Dome camera has a rounded/dome-shaped housing.

It is commonly used in:

* Offices
* Indoor spaces
* Corridors
* Reception areas
* Commercial buildings

Its design provides a relatively compact and discreet installation.

---

# 5. CCTV Camera Planning

Before the cameras were installed, the technicians already had a **camera plan/layout**.

The technicians were laying cables according to this previously prepared plan.

The camera plan determines things such as:

* Where cameras should be positioned.
* Which areas each camera should cover.
* Where cables should run.
* How the cameras should be distributed throughout the facility.
* How to reduce blind spots.

This showed me that CCTV installation is not simply a matter of putting cameras wherever convenient. There is planning involved before physical installation begins.

---

# 6. Cable Installation

I observed the technicians laying cables for the CCTV cameras according to the camera plan.

The cables provide the required connections for the surveillance system.

For IP-based CCTV systems, Ethernet cables such as Cat5e or Cat6 may be used to transmit network data, and Power over Ethernet (PoE) can allow compatible cameras to receive both power and network connectivity through the same Ethernet cable.

The cable installation therefore forms an important part of the CCTV infrastructure.

Proper cable routing helps to:

* Keep the installation organized.
* Reduce cable damage.
* Ensure cameras can communicate with the network/recording system.
* Make future maintenance easier.
* Follow the planned camera locations.

---

# 7. Fabrication of Camera Mounting Structures

The technicians were also preparing to fabricate iron structures according to templates for the three different camera types.

The templates were used as guides for producing the appropriate mounting structures.

This is necessary because different camera types may require different mounting arrangements.

The fabricated structures can provide:

* Physical support for the cameras.
* Appropriate positioning.
* Stability.
* The correct viewing angle.
* A secure mounting point.

This showed that CCTV installation can involve more than IT/networking. It can also require physical infrastructure, metal fabrication, mounting, and construction work.

---

# 8. Field Work

I also learnt about the difference between **field work** and **administrative work** within a technical organization.

The activities we were carrying out were described as **field work**.

Field work involves physically going to the location where technical work is required and carrying out tasks according to instructions.

Examples from what I observed include:

* Going to another branch.
* Inspecting the installation location.
* Laying CCTV cables.
* Preparing camera mounting structures.
* Installing or preparing equipment.
* Carrying out physical maintenance.
* Working according to an existing technical plan.

Field workers generally execute practical tasks and follow the instructions or plans provided by supervisors, engineers, or administrators.

---

# 9. Administrative Work

I was also taught that administrative/management-oriented technical work involves more planning, decision-making, coordination, supervision, and issuing instructions.

For example, an administrative or supervisory person may be responsible for:

* Planning a CCTV installation.
* Deciding where cameras should be placed.
* Creating or approving the camera layout.
* Determining what equipment and materials are needed.
* Assigning technicians to different tasks.
* Supervising field workers.
* Reviewing completed work.
* Making technical decisions.
* Giving instructions to the field team.
* Managing documentation and reports.

I was told that administrative work was considered harder in terms of responsibility because the person has to think, make decisions, coordinate activities, and take responsibility for the work being carried out.

I also learnt that administrative positions generally receive higher compensation than many field positions because of the additional responsibility and decision-making involved.

However, field work is valuable because it provides practical experience and helps technicians understand how systems are actually installed, maintained, and operated.

---

Below is a detailed, self-contained summary of **everything discussed for SIWES Day 32**, including what you reported, what I explained, the technical concepts involved, and the professional lessons.

## SIWES Day 32 — Comprehensive Summary

### 1. Activities I Personally Carried Out

* I inputted reports as part of the day's office/IT activities.
* The main hands-on task I personally performed was **report entry/documentation**.
* Most of the networking and software-related activities described below were activities I **observed**, rather than directly performed.

---

## 2. LAN Cable and Network Connectivity Work

I observed **Mr P redesigning and reorganizing the LAN cable connectivity in an office**.

The purpose of the work was to make the office's network connectivity more efficient and to create enough connectivity capacity for **one additional device**, despite the office having limited available Ethernet ports.

The office had:

* **4 Ethernet ports/faceplate ports**, but only 3 were functioning.
* **2 Avaya IP phones**, although one Avaya had a damaged Ethernet port, leaving only 3 usable ports on the phones.
* **1 wired printer**
* **1 desktop computer**
* **1 laptop**
* **1 smart TV**

This meant there were approximately **6 devices requiring network connectivity**, while the available infrastructure had limited usable ports.

Mr P reorganized the LAN connections and cable arrangement to use the available infrastructure more efficiently.

### Network resource optimization

This demonstrated the practical concept of **network resource optimization**: making better use of existing ports, cables, and network equipment instead of immediately installing additional infrastructure.

The work involved concepts related to:

* LAN connectivity
* Ethernet ports
* Structured cabling
* Network endpoint connectivity
* Port management
* Cable organization
* Infrastructure optimization
* Troubleshooting limited network resources

---

## 3. Avaya and Ethernet Port Sharing

I observed the use of **Avaya IP phones to improve connectivity when there were not enough Ethernet ports available on the wall faceplate**.

An Avaya IP phone can have a network connection going into the phone and another Ethernet connection going from the phone to a computer. This allows a single wall/network connection to serve both the IP phone and another network device.

Conceptually:

**Wall Ethernet port → Avaya phone → Computer**

Instead of requiring:

**Wall port → Avaya phone**

and another separate:

**Wall port → Computer**

This can be useful when there are limited Ethernet ports.

The observation taught me that network engineers do not always have to install new cabling or add new wall ports immediately. Existing infrastructure can sometimes be reorganized or utilized more efficiently.

---

## 4. Geniac Security Software Deployment

I also observed the deployment of **Geniac**, described as new software intended to improve security.

The software was being deployed to users' PCs **from the backend/centrally**, rather than requiring the IT team to manually install it on every individual computer.

This demonstrated concepts related to:

* Centralized software deployment
* Endpoint management
* Enterprise IT administration
* Security software deployment
* Backend/centralized management
* Remote administration

The deployment caused some users to contact the IT team because they noticed the changes and wanted to confirm that the software was safe.

---

## 5. User Awareness and IT Support

One of the things that stood out to me was how observant the users were.

Users noticed that something had changed on their computers and contacted IT to ask questions and express concerns.

This showed me that IT staff should not assume that users will ignore changes to their systems. Users may notice:

* New applications
* Security software
* Unexpected system changes
* New prompts or notifications
* Changes in computer behavior

The IT team responded professionally even though **HQ was responsible for the backend deployment rather than the local IT team**.

They were apologetic about the inconvenience and took action to address the users' concerns.

They also ensured that **emails would be sent to users to inform them about the deployment/change**.

This demonstrated the importance of:

* User communication
* Change management
* Customer service in IT
* Escalation
* Professionalism
* Reassurance
* Clear communication during system changes

---

## 6. Remote Access and Permission

An additional professional lesson I noted was:

**Permission should be obtained before taking remote control of a user's computer.**

Even when IT personnel have the technical ability to remotely access a system, doing so without informing the user can make the user uncomfortable or offended.

A professional remote-support process should generally involve:

1. Informing the user that remote access is required.
2. Explaining why access is needed.
3. Obtaining the user's permission.
4. Connecting to the system.
5. Carrying out the necessary troubleshooting or maintenance.
6. Ending the remote session when the work is complete.

This relates to:

* User privacy
* IT ethics
* Professional conduct
* Trust
* Security
* Remote support practices

DAY 33
## SIWES Day 33 – Wednesday

I went with Mr. Felix to work on two cameras at IBM whose problems were not yet known. We took along tools such as a crimping tool, stripper, plier, flat screwdriver, star screwdriver, LAN tester, and a PoE splitter.

The first camera problem was traced to the switch. Only two ports on the switch were working, while three cameras were connected to it. Before we confirmed that the switch was the problem, we recrimped the camera cable, tested it with the LAN tester, and also tried the PoE splitter.

For the second camera, there was no major fault. It only required removing and reconnecting the cable properly before it started working again.

To solve the switch issue for the first camera, a switch from another location that only had two cameras connected to it was removed and exchanged with the faulty switch.

During the exchange process, I observed the need to be careful with the uplink and downlink connections. I understood that the uplink port brings internet or network connection from one area or switch, while the downlink sends the connection to another switch or device.

I also understood why the exchange was done. Another five cameras in a different area had gone offline because the switch connected to them was not receiving internet from the uplink of the faulty switch that was replaced.

During the replacement, I noticed devices called SFP modules inserted into special ports on the switch. The fibre patch cables were connected through these SFP ports. We had to exchange the SFP modules as well while changing the switch because they were configured to work with the fibre patch cables.

Another thing I observed was that Mr. Felix already knew the camera locations, the switches they were connected to, and where the uplink and downlink connections came from. This showed me the importance of understanding network structure and planning in CCTV and networking environments.

Your observations were good because you did more than just describe the tasks — you explained the troubleshooting process and what you learnt from it. The report also shows growth in understanding networking concepts such as uplink, downlink, switches, PoE splitters, SFP modules, and fibre patch cables.

A strong part of the report was how you explained the process used to identify the faulty switch. You mentioned testing the cable, recrimping, using a LAN tester, and trying the PoE splitter before concluding that the switch was the issue. That shows logical troubleshooting rather than guessing.

Another good observation was your explanation of the network structure. Noticing how Mr. Felix already understood the camera locations, uplink/downlink paths, and switch connections shows awareness of how important network mapping and planning are in field work.

One improvement is grammar and sentence structure. Some parts were difficult to read because several ideas were combined into one sentence. Separating ideas into shorter sentences makes the report clearer and more professional.

You can also improve by using more technical terms consistently. For example:

* “internet” can sometimes be replaced with “network connection”
* “camera cable” can be specified as “Ethernet cable” if correct
* “2 port switch” should be written as “2-port switch”

Overall, the report was detailed, practical, and showed good technical observation for SIWES field work.
For **Day 33**, the tasks you personally carried out and observed include:

### Tasks Performed

* Accompanied Mr. Felix to troubleshoot two faulty CCTV cameras.
* Gathered and used troubleshooting tools such as:

  * Crimping tool
  * LAN tester
  * PoE splitter
  * Screwdrivers
  * Pliers
* Recrimped a camera network cable.
* Tested network cables using a LAN tester.
* Tested camera connectivity using a PoE splitter.
* Removed and reinserted connections on one camera, restoring its operation.
* Assisted in diagnosing a faulty network switch.
* Observed and participated in the replacement of a faulty switch.
* Ensured correct uplink and downlink connections during switch replacement.
* Observed the removal and reinstallation of SFP modules and fibre patch cable connections.

### Knowledge Gained

* Learned the difference between uplink and downlink ports.
* Learned how a faulty switch can cause multiple cameras to go offline.
* Learned the function of SFP modules and fibre patch cables in network connectivity.
* Understood the importance of network topology knowledge when troubleshooting CCTV systems.

DAY 35
## SIWES Day 35 — Complete Summary

### What happened

* We went to another branch of the company, **ISM**, to troubleshoot a display/HDMI transmission problem.
* The equipment involved was identified as a **200m HDMI Extender**.
* The system consisted of two paired devices:

  * **Transmitter (TX)**
  * **Receiver (RX)**
* The purpose of the system is to transmit **HDMI video/audio signals over a long distance**, using an Ethernet/network cable between the transmitter and receiver.
* The receiver was not successfully transmitting/displaying the signal to the **TV**.

### Troubleshooting performed

* We carried out a series of tests to determine why the HDMI signal was not reaching the TV.
* During the troubleshooting, the **transmitter unit** was identified as the likely source of the problem.
* The **power adapter/charger connected to the transmitter had been burnt**.
* Further inspection suggested that the **transmitter itself may also have been damaged/burnt**.
* I learned that the transmitter and receiver are designed to work as a **paired set**.
* I was told that if one of the units becomes faulty, the manufacturer/system may require the **complete transmitter-and-receiver set to be replaced**, rather than purchasing only the faulty transmitter.

### Ports I observed

The transmitter and receiver had several ports/interfaces:

* **HDMI port** — used for HDMI video/audio connection.
* **Two USB ports** — likely related to KVM functionality, such as extending the use of a **keyboard and mouse** between the source and display locations.
* **Ethernet/RJ45 port** — used to carry the HDMI signal between the transmitter and receiver over a network cable, potentially over distances of up to **200 m**, depending on the extender model and installation.
* **DC power input** — the cylindrical/barrel-type connector with a central pin, used to supply power to the device.
* **Another unidentified port** — its function was not established during the visit. It could potentially be an IR-related interface or another feature depending on the exact model, but this was not confirmed.

### What you learned

* How a **200m HDMI Extender** works.
* The difference between the **transmitter and receiver** roles.
* That HDMI extenders can use **Ethernet cables to carry HDMI signals over long distances**.
* The importance of checking the **power supply/adapter** when troubleshooting electronic/network equipment.
* How a damaged power adapter can potentially cause or accompany damage to the connected equipment.
* That some transmitter/receiver systems are **paired**, meaning replacing one component individually may not be appropriate.
* Basic identification of the different ports and interfaces on an HDMI extender.
* Practical troubleshooting involves systematically testing different components rather than immediately replacing the entire system.

### What you actually did/participated in

From what you described, your SIWES activity can be summarized as:

* Visited the ISM branch with the IT team.
* **Observed and participated in troubleshooting** a 200m HDMI Extender that was failing to display the signal on a TV.
* Observed the testing of the **transmitter and receiver** to isolate the fault.
* Helped/observed the identification of a **burnt power adapter** and suspected damage to the transmitter.
* Examined the physical interfaces on the transmitter and receiver.
* Learned the function of the HDMI, USB, Ethernet, and power connections.
* Learned about the paired nature of the transmitter and receiver.
* Gained practical exposure to **hardware fault diagnosis and HDMI signal transmission systems**.

### Important technical correction/clarification

The Ethernet port on this particular device **does not necessarily mean the extender is providing internet/network access**. In a typical HDMI-over-Cat extender, the RJ45 connection is being used as the physical medium for transporting the HDMI signal from the TX to the RX.

So the basic signal path is:

**HDMI Source → TX (Transmitter) → Ethernet/Cat cable → RX (Receiver) → HDMI → TV**

And the power adapters supply power to the respective units.


DAY 36
Below is a comprehensive, copyable summary of everything discussed about **SIWES Day 36**, including your original observations and the additional explanations.

# SIWES Day 36 — Comprehensive Information Summary

## 1. Main Activity

The main activity for SIWES Day 36 was a **training/learning session on the OSI (Open Systems Interconnection) Model and its relationship to network troubleshooting**.

There were no major hands-on installation or maintenance tasks recalled for the day. The primary role was that of a **trainee/observer**, learning how the OSI model can be used to understand network communication and systematically troubleshoot IT problems.

---

# 2. OSI Model

The OSI model consists of **seven layers**. It provides a conceptual framework for understanding how data moves between devices and applications and is particularly useful when troubleshooting network problems.

The seven layers from bottom to top are:

1. Physical
2. Data Link
3. Network
4. Transport
5. Session
6. Presentation
7. Application

A key lesson was that different types of network problems can be associated with different OSI layers. Instead of troubleshooting randomly, a technician can use the model to narrow down where a failure is occurring.

---

## Layer 1 — Physical Layer

The Physical Layer deals with the **physical transmission of data**.

Examples mentioned:

* USB cables
* Ethernet/LAN cables
* Fibre-optic cables
* Physical network connections

A problem with a damaged Ethernet cable, disconnected cable, faulty connector, or physical connection can therefore be investigated at Layer 1.

A **LAN cable tester** can be used to test whether an Ethernet cable is properly terminated and whether its conductors are functioning correctly.

### Example

If a computer cannot connect to the network:

**Check the cable → test the cable with a LAN tester → check the physical network connection.**

---

# 3. Layer 2 — Data Link Layer

The Data Link Layer deals with communication over the local network and the movement of data between devices on the same network segment.

You noted that this was related to **ports used to transmit data**.

A more precise understanding is that Layer 2 deals with things such as:

* Ethernet frames
* MAC addresses
* Network interface cards
* Switches
* VLANs
* Local network communication

A network switch primarily operates at this layer.

### Example

If an Ethernet cable is physically working but a computer still cannot communicate through a particular switch port, the technician may investigate:

* Switch port status
* Ethernet connection
* MAC address learning
* VLAN assignment
* Network interface status

---

# 4. Layer 3 — Network Layer

The Network Layer is responsible for **logical addressing and routing data between networks**.

You described it as the layer that tells the data its **source and destination**.

The major concept here is **IP addressing**.

Examples:

* Source IP address
* Destination IP address
* Routers
* Routing
* Subnets

### Example

If a printer has an IP address such as:

`192.168.1.50`

and your computer is trying to reach it, the Network Layer helps determine how packets should get from the computer to the printer.

Tools such as **ping** can help determine whether the destination is reachable.

---

# 5. Layer 4 — Transport Layer

The Transport Layer controls communication between applications running on different devices.

The two major protocols discussed were:

* **TCP — Transmission Control Protocol**
* **UDP — User Datagram Protocol**

### TCP

TCP provides reliable, connection-oriented communication.

It is useful when data must arrive correctly and in order.

Examples include:

* Web communication
* File transfers
* Email
* Many application protocols

### UDP

UDP is connectionless and generally has less overhead than TCP.

It is useful when speed is more important than guaranteed delivery.

Examples include:

* Streaming
* Voice/video communication
* Some online games
* DNS queries

Transport-layer communication is also closely associated with **port numbers**.

Examples:

* HTTP — 80
* HTTPS — 443
* SSH — 22
* DNS — 53

---

# 6. Layer 5 — Session Layer

The Session Layer manages **communication sessions between applications**.

You related this to sessions on websites.

A session represents an ongoing communication interaction between systems or applications.

You also noted that sessions can be investigated using tools such as:

* Wireshark
* ShowMyIP

The important idea is that when two systems communicate, there is more happening than simply having an IP address. Applications establish and maintain communication sessions.

---

# 7. Layer 6 — Presentation Layer

The Presentation Layer deals with how data is **represented, encoded, encrypted, and compressed** so that systems can interpret or efficiently transmit it.

Topics you mentioned included:

* Encoding
* Encryption
* Compression

### Compression example

You compared this to sending images or videos through WhatsApp.

A video or image may be compressed so that it requires less storage and bandwidth.

This can reduce file size but may also reduce quality.

### Encryption

Encryption transforms readable information into a form that unauthorized people cannot easily understand.

The receiving system uses the appropriate mechanism to convert it back into usable information.

---

# 8. Layer 7 — Application Layer

The Application Layer is the layer closest to the **end user and applications**.

It provides network-related services that applications and users interact with.

Examples include:

* Web browsers
* Email applications
* File-transfer applications
* Web applications
* Network services

Your instructor emphasized that **Layers 5–7 are particularly important for programming**, because programmers work heavily with application communication, sessions, data representation, and user-facing software.

---

# 9. Why the OSI Model Matters in Troubleshooting

One of the major lessons from the session was that the OSI model can provide a **structured approach to troubleshooting**.

Instead of simply guessing what is wrong, a technician can identify which layer is most likely responsible.

For example:

### Cable problem

Likely:

**Layer 1 — Physical**

### Switch/VLAN/local Ethernet problem

Likely:

**Layer 2 — Data Link**

### IP addressing/routing problem

Likely:

**Layer 3 — Network**

### TCP/UDP or port-related problem

Likely:

**Layer 4 — Transport**

### Session/application communication problem

Likely:

**Layers 5–7**

This makes troubleshooting more systematic.

---

# 10. Troubleshooting Techniques You Learned

Several troubleshooting approaches were discussed.

## 1. Top-Down Approach

Start from:

**Layer 7 → Layer 6 → Layer 5 → Layer 4 → Layer 3 → Layer 2 → Layer 1**

You begin with the application/user-facing problem and work downward toward the physical equipment.

This can be useful when the physical network appears to be functioning but an application is not working.

---

## 2. Bottom-Up Approach

Start from:

**Layer 1 → Layer 2 → Layer 3 → Layer 4 → Layer 5 → Layer 6 → Layer 7**

You begin with the physical connection and progressively move upward.

For example:

**Cable → Switch → IP → Transport → Application**

This is useful when you suspect a basic connectivity problem.

---

## 3. Divide and Conquer

Instead of checking every component one after another, you test a point somewhere in the middle to determine which side of the system contains the problem.

For example:

**PC → Switch → Router → Server**

You could test communication with the router.

If the PC can reach the router, the lower portion of the network is probably functioning, so you can focus your investigation on the path beyond the router.

This helps narrow down the problem quickly.

---

## 4. Experience-Based Troubleshooting

You mentioned that your instructor discussed a method involving a problem that the technician has **encountered before or already has knowledge of**.

The general idea is:

If you recognize the symptoms from a previous incident, you can use your previous knowledge to identify and resolve the problem faster.

For example, if a technician has previously encountered a particular printer error and knows its cause, they can immediately investigate the known solution instead of starting from scratch.

---

## 5. Online Research / Third-Party Method

When the technician does not know the cause of a problem, they can research it using external resources.

Examples include:

* Official documentation
* Technical manuals
* Vendor support pages
* Forums
* Knowledge bases
* Search engines
* Technical communities

This is particularly useful for unfamiliar errors and equipment.

---

# 11. Network Troubleshooting Example

A common PC network problem discussed was when a computer appears to be **asking for or having an IP-related problem**.

A technician can investigate progressively:

### Application/user side

Determine what the user is experiencing.

### Network configuration

Check whether the PC has a valid IP address.

### Data Link

Check the LAN connection and switch connection.

### Physical

Check the Ethernet cable and test it with a LAN tester if necessary.

This demonstrates how the OSI model can guide troubleshooting.

---

# 12. Printer Troubleshooting

A printer problem can occur at several OSI layers.

### Application Layer

Possible issues:

* Printing application problem
* Print job problem
* User-specific issue

First determine whether the problem affects:

**Only one user or everybody connected to the printer?**

This is an important troubleshooting question because it helps determine whether the issue is with the individual PC or with the printer/network itself.

### Physical Layer

Check:

* Power
* Cables
* Physical connections
* Paper
* Paper jams

You mentioned printer problems such as:

* No paper
* Printer jam
* Cable problem
* Blinking indicator light

### Data Link Layer

Check the printer's network cable and local network connection.

### Network Layer

Check:

* Printer IP address
* Whether the printer is reachable
* Whether the PC can communicate with the printer

One useful test is attempting to **access or ping the printer using its IP address**.

For example:

`ping <printer-IP>`

If the printer responds, network connectivity to the printer is likely functioning.

---

# 13. Email Troubleshooting

Another example discussed was an email application showing that it is **not connected or disconnected**.

Possible troubleshooting steps include:

1. Check whether the computer has a valid IP address.
2. Confirm network connectivity.
3. Close and reopen the email application.
4. Log out and back into the application if appropriate.
5. Restart the computer if necessary.

The key lesson is not to immediately assume the email application itself is faulty. The underlying network connection may be the actual cause.

---

# 14. Slow/Lagging Computer Troubleshooting

You also discussed problems involving a computer that is **slow or lagging**.

Possible causes include:

### Temporary files

Accumulated temporary files can contribute to unnecessary storage usage and system clutter.

A technician may clean temporary files as part of routine maintenance.

### RAM problems

Insufficient or faulty RAM can cause poor system performance, particularly when multiple applications are running.

### Corrupted files

Attempting to open corrupted files can sometimes cause an application or system to behave abnormally.

The technician therefore needs to determine whether the lag is caused by:

* Hardware
* Software
* Storage
* Memory
* Corrupted files
* Background processes

---

# 15. Important Troubleshooting Questions

A technician should not immediately start changing settings or replacing equipment.

Useful questions include:

* What exactly is the problem?
* When did the problem begin?
* Has it happened before?
* Did anything change before the problem started?
* Is only one user affected?
* Are multiple users affected?
* Can another computer perform the same task?
* Can the affected computer access other network resources?
* Is the problem hardware or software related?

For example, if only one person cannot print but everyone else can, the problem is more likely to be related to that user's computer, configuration, driver, or connection.

If everyone cannot print, the problem may be with the printer or shared network connection.

---

# 16. Tools Mentioned or Relevant to the Lesson

Tools and technologies connected to the topics you learned include:

* **LAN tester** — tests Ethernet cable wiring/connectivity
* **Wireshark** — captures and analyzes network traffic
* **Ping** — tests IP connectivity/reachability
* **IP configuration tools** — inspect a device's network configuration
* **ShowMyIP** — displays public IP-related information
* **Printer IP address** — allows network-based printer troubleshooting
* **Switches** — provide local network connectivity

---

# 17. Overall Practical Lesson

The most important lesson from Day 36 was that **troubleshooting should be systematic rather than based on random guessing**.

The OSI model gives an IT technician a framework for asking:

> "At which layer is the problem occurring?"

Once the likely layer is identified, the technician can investigate the relevant equipment, protocol, configuration, or application.

For example:

**User cannot print**

→ Is the printer application working?

→ Is the printer powered on?

→ Is the cable connected?

→ Is the switch/network connection working?

→ Does the printer have an IP address?

→ Can the PC reach the printer's IP?

→ Can the print service/application communicate with the printer?

This transforms a vague complaint such as **"the printer isn't working"** into a structured troubleshooting process.

DAY 37
Here is a comprehensive extraction of everything discussed for **SIWES Day 37**, including your original observations and the technical interpretation added during the discussion:

## SIWES Day 37 — Detailed Summary

### Main Activity

I returned to continue and complete the troubleshooting work that had started on **SIWES Day 35**. The task involved an **NVR (Network Video Recorder), a 200 m IP/HDMI transmitter and receiver system, and a Smart TV/display**.

A new transmitter and receiver device was obtained and tested because the previous setup had not been working properly.

### 1. Testing the New Transmitter and Receiver

The new **200 m transmitter and receiver** was connected and tested. However, the same problem from the previous setup occurred.

The **receiver continued searching for a transmission/signal** instead of displaying the video feed. This indicated that the receiver was not receiving a usable video signal from the transmitter.

The troubleshooting therefore shifted from simply replacing the transmitter/receiver to checking the equipment supplying the video signal.

### 2. Troubleshooting the NVR HDMI Output

During the investigation, it was discovered that the **HDMI output port on the NVR appeared to have been burnt or damaged**.

The implication was that the transmitter itself might not have been the actual problem. If the NVR was not producing an HDMI video signal through the relevant port, there would be nothing for the transmitter to capture and transmit to the receiver.

After further testing and changing things around, one of the NVR's HDMI outputs eventually picked up a signal.

However, this required making **changes to the HDMI/output-port configuration in the NVR's software/settings**.

### 3. Successful Display on the Smart TV

After the NVR output began producing a signal, the **receiver was finally able to display the video on the Smart TV**.

This confirmed that the transmitter and receiver could work when they received an appropriate video signal from the NVR.

However, another problem became apparent: **the display resolution was not working correctly**.

### 4. Resolution/Compatibility Problem

The setup had problems with **1080p resolution**. Other/lower resolution settings were also tested, but there were still compatibility problems.

The explanation given was related to the resolution being **too low or otherwise incompatible with the camera/video feed**.

This demonstrated that getting a physical video signal through the system was not necessarily enough. The **source resolution, camera output, NVR settings, transmitter/receiver capabilities, and display/TV compatibility** also needed to match.

### 5. NVR USB/Mouse Problem

While working with the new IP device/setup, another fault was discovered.

The **mouse connected to the NVR was not responding**.

Further investigation showed that the **USB port on the NVR appeared to have been burnt/damaged as well**.

This meant that the problem was not limited to the HDMI output. The NVR also had a faulty USB interface, making it difficult to control the NVR locally using a mouse.

### 6. Alternative Control Method — iVMS/VMS

Because the NVR's USB port was not functioning, an alternative method of accessing and controlling the surveillance system was considered.

The solution was to use **iVMS/VMS software on a user's desktop computer**.

Instead of relying on the physical mouse connected directly to the NVR, the desktop could be used to access and monitor the camera/NVR system through the network.

### What I Personally Did/Observed

My involvement for the day included:

* Returned to continue the unfinished troubleshooting task from Day 35.
* Observed the testing of a **new 200 m IP transmitter and receiver**.
* Observed that the receiver continued searching for a transmission.
* Participated in/observed troubleshooting to determine whether the transmitter/receiver was actually the source of the problem.
* Observed the discovery that an **NVR HDMI port appeared to be burnt/damaged**.
* Observed testing and switching of the NVR's HDMI outputs.
* Observed changes being made to the **NVR software/configuration** to change the active output port.
* Confirmed that the receiver eventually displayed the video feed on a **Smart TV**.
* Observed problems with **1080p and other resolution settings** and learned that video resolution compatibility is important between cameras, NVRs, transmission equipment, and displays.
* Observed that the **NVR USB port was also faulty**, preventing the connected mouse from working.
* Learned that when the NVR's local controls are unavailable, **iVMS/VMS software on a networked desktop** can be used as an alternative for monitoring/accessing the surveillance system.

### Technical Concepts Learned

**NVR (Network Video Recorder):**
A device used to receive, record, manage, and provide access to video streams from IP surveillance cameras.

**HDMI output:**
Carries the video/audio output from the NVR to a display or another video-transmission device. If the HDMI output is faulty, the transmitter may have no video signal to transmit.

**Transmitter and receiver:**
The transmitter sends the video signal over the required transmission medium/network, while the receiver receives the signal and outputs it to a display such as a Smart TV.

**Resolution compatibility:**
The camera/NVR output resolution needs to be compatible with the transmitter/receiver and the display. A successful physical connection does not guarantee that the video will display correctly.

**USB interface:**
The NVR's USB port can be used to connect peripherals such as a mouse for local control. A damaged USB port prevents normal local mouse operation.

**iVMS/VMS:**
Video management software can provide network-based access to surveillance equipment, allowing users to monitor/manage cameras without depending entirely on the NVR's local mouse and display interface.

### Overall Lesson

The major lesson from the day was that troubleshooting a surveillance system requires checking the **entire signal path**, rather than immediately assuming that the newly replaced device is faulty.

The signal path can be viewed broadly as:

**Camera → NVR → HDMI output → Transmitter → Receiver → Smart TV**

A failure at any point can prevent the final video from appearing. In this case, replacing the transmitter/receiver did not immediately solve the problem because the NVR itself had **damaged HDMI and USB ports**. Troubleshooting eventually involved changing the NVR output configuration, testing different ports, checking resolution compatibility, and using **iVMS/VMS on a desktop as an alternative access method**.

### Important Clarification

I did **not** establish exactly why the NVR's USB port and HDMI port were damaged, nor did I establish that the transmitter/receiver caused the damage. The observation was that the NVR had faulty/burnt ports while the transmission system was being troubleshot. The relationship between the two should therefore not be stated as a confirmed cause-and-effect relationship.

**SIWES Day 39 — Complete Summary**

### Original Experience / Activities

* I went with **Mr P and Mr F** to carry out **CCTV/camera maintenance**.
* I mainly **observed the maintenance activities** and learned from the procedures being performed.
* Some of the cameras were installed at **significant heights or difficult-to-reach locations**, so the technicians had to climb and position themselves carefully to reach them.
* In some locations, the cameras were positioned where **normal ladders could not easily reach**, making the maintenance work more physically demanding and requiring careful access to the equipment.

### Preventive Maintenance

* One important thing I learned was the purpose of **preventive maintenance**.
* Maintenance is not only about waiting for equipment to develop faults and then repairing it.
* The purpose of preventive maintenance is to **identify and address potential problems before they become actual failures**.
* For CCTV systems, regular maintenance can help prevent issues such as poor image quality, cable faults, loose camera mounts, accumulated dust, and other equipment failures.
* This helped me understand the difference between **preventing a problem** and simply **correcting a problem after it has occurred**.

### Network Rack / Switch Maintenance

* We used **air blowers** to remove accumulated dust from network racks.
* The racks contained **network switches and other networking equipment**.
* Dust removal is important because accumulated dust can affect equipment cleanliness and potentially contribute to overheating or poor equipment conditions.
* The maintenance therefore included not only the cameras themselves but also the **network infrastructure supporting the CCTV system**.

### Camera Lens Cleaning

* We cleaned the **camera lenses** as part of the maintenance.
* A **cleaning spray and rag/cloth** were used to remove dirt and other substances from the lenses.
* Keeping the lens clean is important because dirt or dust on the lens can reduce the clarity of the CCTV footage and affect image quality.

### Camera Mount / Physical Inspection

* We inspected the **physical supports, mounts, and other structures holding the cameras**.
* The purpose was to check whether the structures were still **strong, stable, and fit to continue supporting the cameras**.
* This is important because a loose, damaged, or weakened camera mount could cause the camera to shift position, fall, or stop providing the intended coverage.

### Cable Inspection and Recrimping

* We also inspected the **camera/network cables** for signs of damage.
* Where a cable or connector was found to have a problem, it could require **recrimping**.
* Recrimping involves properly terminating the cable again with a connector when the existing termination is damaged or unreliable.
* This is important for maintaining reliable network connectivity between CCTV cameras and the rest of the surveillance system.

### Technical Knowledge / Lessons Learned

Day 39 exposed me to the practical side of **preventive CCTV maintenance**. I learned that maintaining a surveillance system involves much more than simply checking whether cameras are displaying images.

The maintenance process included:

1. Physically accessing cameras installed at elevated or difficult locations.
2. Cleaning camera lenses.
3. Inspecting camera mounts and physical supports.
4. Checking cables and connectors for damage.
5. Identifying cables that may require recrimping.
6. Cleaning network racks and switches using air blowers.
7. Preventing potential faults through regular inspection rather than waiting for equipment to fail.

I also observed that CCTV maintenance involves both **physical/electrical/network considerations**. A camera can have problems because of its lens, mounting structure, cable, connector, network connection, environmental conditions, or other supporting equipment.

This provided practical exposure to the process of dealing with CCTV faults rather than simply replacing equipment immediately. The activity involved observing the equipment's behavior and checking whether the camera was operating as expected.

Potential troubleshooting concepts involved:

* Identifying non-functioning cameras.
* Checking camera behavior.
* Determining whether a camera is responding correctly.
* Recognizing abnormal camera behavior.
* Understanding that CCTV faults can involve either the camera itself or its configuration/features.

---

### 4. CCTV Intrusion/Tamper Alert Feature

A major new concept learned on Day 40 was the **intrusion/tamper alert feature of CCTV cameras**.

The observation was that when the camera's view is deliberately obstructed or a blind spot is introduced, the camera can detect the situation and send an alert to the surveillance system.

For example, if someone deliberately blocks the camera or interferes with its field of view, the camera can recognize the change and trigger its intrusion/tamper detection mechanism.

A faint **ticking sound** can be heard from the camera when close enough. This sound serves as an indication that the camera has detected the obstruction/interference and has sent an alert to the surveillance system.

The observation was therefore:

**Camera view intentionally obstructed → camera detects the intrusion/obstruction → alert is generated/sent to surveillance system → faint ticking sound may be heard locally.**

---

### 5. Interpreting the Absence of the Alert Sound

Another important observation was what it could mean if the expected alert/ticking sound no longer occurs.

If the same obstruction or blind-spot condition is introduced but the camera no longer produces the expected alert indication, two possible explanations were identified:

1. **The intrusion/tamper alert feature may have been disabled**, possibly through configuration/settings.
2. **The camera may have become weak or developed a fault**, meaning the feature is no longer functioning as expected.

This means that the absence of the expected alert response can itself be useful during troubleshooting.

Rather than assuming the camera is completely dead, the technician can investigate whether:

* The feature has been disabled.
* The camera's functionality has deteriorated.
* The camera requires further troubleshooting or replacement.

DAY 41
Here is an extensive, structured summary of everything discussed for **SIWES Day 41**, including your original details and the interpretation added in my response:

## SIWES Day 41 — Full Summary

### Main Activity: Store Department Systems and Remote Support

I followed **Tumise** to the **Store Department**, where there were **eight computer systems** that needed to be prepared for remote IT support. The purpose was to make sure the systems had all the required software applications needed for the network and remote troubleshooting process.

We checked the eight systems and assisted in making sure that **all four required applications for the network to work properly were installed on every system**. Having these applications installed was important because the IT team needed to be able to **remotely take control/access the computers** in order to troubleshoot and resolve problems affecting users' access to **Navision (Microsoft Dynamics NAV)**.

### Navision Access Problem

There was also a significant issue involving **Navision** during the day.

A **sudden Navision update** was introduced, and the update caused a considerable number of problems with users' access to the application. Because of the amount of trouble the update was creating, the changes were eventually **reverted/resolved by rolling back the update or associated changes**.

As part of troubleshooting the Navision access problem, the **IP address used to access Navision was changed**.

However, changing the IP did not completely solve the problem.

### Shared Credentials / Windows Profile Issue

Before the issue, some users were able to access Navision on their systems using **their colleagues' Navision credentials/IDs**.

After the IP address was changed, these users could no longer access Navision in the same way, even though they had previously been able to do so.

Further investigation showed that the problem was related to the **original Windows user/profile associated with the credentials**. It was discovered that the Navision access worked only when the **original Windows installation/user environment containing the appropriate credentials** was being used.

In other words, simply having the credentials or using the same credentials on another Windows user/system was not sufficient. The original Windows environment was linked in some way to the successful Navision authentication/access.

### Reversion of the Changes

Because the Navision update and subsequent changes were causing too many problems for users, the changes were eventually **reverted**.

This restored the previous configuration and allowed users to regain the access they had before the problematic update/change.

### Technical Concepts Observed

The day's activities exposed me to several practical IT concepts:

* **Remote computer access:** IT personnel can remotely access users' computers to troubleshoot problems without necessarily being physically present at each workstation.
* **Required network/software applications:** Certain applications or supporting software may need to be installed and configured on client computers before remote support or network-related operations can work correctly.
* **Navision / Microsoft Dynamics NAV:** A business application used by the organization, particularly by departments such as the Store Department.
* **IP-based application access:** The IP address used to reach a server or application can affect whether users can connect to it.
* **Software/application updates:** An update can unexpectedly introduce compatibility or access problems.
* **User authentication:** Having a username/credential alone may not guarantee access if authentication is also tied to a specific Windows user environment or configuration.
* **Windows user profiles:** Different Windows environments can have different credentials, permissions, configurations, or authentication states.
* **Troubleshooting:** When the initial solution (changing the IP) did not resolve the problem, further investigation was required to identify the actual cause.
* **Rollback/reversion:** When a new update or configuration causes widespread problems, reverting to the previous working configuration can restore normal operation.

### What I Personally Did / Observed

1. Followed **Tumise** to the Store Department.
2. Assisted with checking the **eight computers** in the department.
3. Helped ensure the **four required applications** were installed on all eight systems.
4. Observed why these applications were necessary for the IT team to **remotely access the systems**.
5. Observed the troubleshooting of **Navision access problems**.
6. Observed the effects of a sudden **Navision update**.
7. Observed the change of the **IP address used to access Navision**.
8. Observed that changing the IP did not solve every user's access problem.
9. Observed troubleshooting involving users who previously accessed Navision through **colleagues' credentials**.
10. Observed the discovery that the **original Windows environment/profile associated with the credentials** was required for access.
11. Observed the eventual **reversion of the problematic changes** because of the large number of issues they caused.

### Key Learning Outcome

Day 41 provided practical exposure to **IT support, remote system administration, application troubleshooting, network configuration, Windows user environments, authentication, and software-update management**.

A major lesson was that an application access problem is not necessarily caused by the network alone. Even after changing the IP address, access remained unavailable for some users because the problem involved the **Windows user environment and authentication configuration**. I also learnt that software updates should be carefully tested because a change intended to improve or update a system can sometimes introduce unexpected problems across multiple users. When a new configuration causes significant disruption, reverting to a previously stable configuration can be an appropriate troubleshooting and recovery approach.
