## SIWES Day 43 – Comprehensive Summary

### Activities and Experience

On SIWES Day 43, I followed **Mr. Israel** to install a **HP Neverstop printer**. The printer was mainly designed for printing and did not have the additional multifunction features of some other office printers.

The installation itself went relatively smoothly, but there was an issue with the **network cable/network connection**. At first, the connection appeared to be acting strangely, which could have suggested that the cable or physical network connection was faulty. However, it was eventually discovered that the problem was not necessarily the cable itself. The issue was related to the company's **Geniac network access/control system**.

I learnt that in a corporate environment, a device cannot simply be connected to the company's network and expected to work. Network access is controlled, and devices may need to be properly authorized or registered before they are allowed to communicate with the organization's network resources.

### Enterprise Network Security Observation

I also learnt an important piece of information concerning **external or unauthorized computers** brought into the company.

If an outside PC is brought into the company and connected to the corporate network using an Ethernet/network cable, the PC may not be granted network access. More importantly, the network team can also be **notified or alerted that an unauthorized device has attempted to connect**.

This showed me that the company's network has mechanisms for controlling and monitoring devices that attempt to gain access. The purpose is to prevent unauthorized devices from accessing company systems, resources, or data and to maintain control over the organization's network.

### Technical Knowledge Gained

From the experience, I learnt about:

* Installing and setting up an office/network printer.
* Connecting a printer to an organization's network.
* Troubleshooting network connectivity problems during device installation.
* The difference between a physical network problem and an access-control problem.
* Enterprise network access control.
* Device authorization and network registration.
* Monitoring of devices attempting to connect to a corporate network.
* How unauthorized devices can be restricted from accessing organizational network resources.
* The importance of network security in an organization.

### Broader Realization

This experience also contributed to a broader realization about IT for me. The more I discover during my SIWES placement, the more interested I become in continuing to learn rather than stopping at the basic things I already know.

I am beginning to realize that IT in a real organization is much bigger than simply repairing computers, installing software, configuring devices, or connecting cables. There are different layers of control and administration operating behind the systems that ordinary users interact with.

There are administrators, network teams, security controls, organizational policies, system configurations, software updates, infrastructure decisions, and other levels of authority that can affect how IT systems behave.

For example, something that appears to be a simple network-cable problem may actually be caused by an access-control policy. A software update can affect an entire system. A change made by someone with higher administrative privileges can change how users access a service.

This has made me more curious about the deeper layers of IT—particularly how networks, security, infrastructure, administration, and organizational systems work together.

### Overall Summary

On Day 43, I assisted Mr. Israel with the installation of a **HP Neverstop printer** and gained practical experience troubleshooting a network connection during the setup. I discovered that the apparent network issue was connected to the organization's **Geniac network access controls**, rather than simply being a faulty cable.

I also learnt that external or unauthorized PCs connected to the company's network can be blocked from accessing network resources and that the network team may be alerted when such a device attempts to connect.

The experience gave me practical knowledge of **printer installation, network troubleshooting, enterprise network access control, device authorization, and network security**. More importantly, it increased my interest in IT because I am discovering that behind the devices and applications I interact with are deeper systems, policies, security mechanisms, and administrative controls that can significantly affect the entire IT environment.



DAY 44
Here is a comprehensive summary of everything from **SIWES Day 44**, including your original observations and the technical points I added:

## SIWES Day 44 — Comprehensive Summary

### Activities and Observations

On Day 44 of my SIWES, I followed **Mr. Y** to troubleshoot a computer system that was receiving a network connection from the **wrong network series**. The issue involved the system being placed on a different network range from the one it was expected to use.

During the troubleshooting process, I learnt more about how network switches are configured and how the configuration of individual switch ports can affect the network a connected device receives.

I discovered that **each port on a network switch can be configured according to specific network requirements**. Different ports on the same physical switch can be associated with different network segments, hosts, or VLAN configurations. This means that two computers connected to different ports on the same switch may not necessarily receive addresses from the same network series. The configuration applied to the particular switch port can determine which network the connected device belongs to.

This helped me understand that network troubleshooting is not always limited to checking the computer itself. A computer may appear to have a network problem, but the actual cause can be related to the **switch port or the network configuration assigned to that port**.

I also learnt that an **Ethernet cable can be used to establish a direct network connection between a desktop computer and a laptop for file sharing**. Instead of using a flash drive or another external storage device, the two computers can communicate over the Ethernet connection when the appropriate network and file-sharing settings are configured.

### Technical Knowledge Gained

* A network switch can have **different configurations on individual ports**.
* Individual switch ports can be assigned to specific network segments or VLANs.
* A device receiving the wrong IP/network series can sometimes be caused by the **configuration of the switch port** rather than the computer itself.
* Troubleshooting network problems therefore requires checking both the **end device and the network infrastructure**.
* Ethernet cables are not only used to provide internet/network access; they can also establish communication between two computers.
* A desktop and laptop can be connected directly through Ethernet and configured for **file sharing over the network**.
* Direct Ethernet file sharing can eliminate the need for removable storage when transferring files between two systems.

### Main Lesson

The major lesson from Day 44 was that **network connectivity depends on both the configuration of the endpoint and the network infrastructure connecting it**. I gained a better understanding of how switch-port configurations can determine the network environment of a connected computer and also learnt that Ethernet connections can be used for direct communication and file sharing between computers.

**SIWES Day 46 — Extensive Summary**

On Day 46 of my SIWES training, I participated in several IT support, system administration, hardware installation, software management, security, maintenance, and documentation activities.

The activity helped me understand the importance of UPS devices in protecting computers and other IT equipment from power interruptions, fluctuations, and sudden shutdowns. UPS systems can provide temporary backup power and help prevent potential data loss or hardware problems when power is interrupted.

### 2. Removal of Unwanted/Unauthorized Applications

I worked on a PC where I discovered that **several applications had been installed even though they were not supposed to be on the computer**. I uninstalled the unwanted applications.

This showed me that IT support is not only about fixing hardware or connectivity problems but also involves maintaining the software environment of organizational computers. Removing applications that should not be present can help keep systems compliant with organizational policies, reduce unnecessary software, and potentially reduce security risks.

### 3. Creating an Administrator Account and Disabling the Default User

I created a **new administrator account** on a computer and protected it with a password. After creating the new administrator account, I **disabled the default user account**.

This exposed me to a basic aspect of Windows user and account management. It also demonstrated how system administrators can control who has administrative access to a computer. Using a dedicated, password-protected administrator account and disabling unnecessary/default accounts can improve system security and reduce unauthorized access.

### 4. Monitoring Windows Update

I monitored the **Windows Update process** on a computer. This involved observing the system while Windows updates were being processed to ensure that the update operation proceeded properly.

This reinforced the importance of keeping operating systems updated. Windows updates can provide security patches, bug fixes, stability improvements, and other system updates. Monitoring updates is important in an organizational environment because an update can sometimes affect system operation or require troubleshooting if something goes wrong.

### 5. Updating an IT Report

This demonstrated that IT work also involves documentation and record keeping. Reports allow technical activities, observations, maintenance work, and other information to be properly recorded for future reference and organizational tracking.

### 6. Observing AutoCAD Installation

I observed that Trellix had to be **temporarily disabled** to allow the AutoCAD installation to proceed.

This showed me that security software can sometimes interfere with legitimate applications when an application is detected as potentially unsafe or suspicious. It also demonstrated the importance of understanding the interaction between antivirus/security software and legitimate software installations.

However, disabling security software should be done carefully, temporarily, and under appropriate IT supervision because antivirus protection exists to prevent potentially harmful software from running.

### Technical Areas Covered on Day 46

* **Hardware installation:** Installing three UPS units.

DAY 49
Here is a consolidated, detailed record of **everything you reported and everything we established for SIWES Day 49**, written so you can paste it elsewhere for further extraction.

**SIWES Day 49**

### 1. Hikvision NVR and HDD Preparation

I worked on the preparation and configuration of a **Hikvision Network Video Recorder (NVR)** used as part of a CCTV surveillance system.

I worked with **four Hard Disk Drives (HDDs)** that were going to be installed inside the Hikvision NVR. My task was to attach/screw the plastic mounting components onto the four HDDs so that they could be properly secured and fitted into the NVR.

This activity introduced me more clearly to the role of storage in CCTV systems. I initially did not realize that the NVR used HDDs for storage or how much physical storage space a surveillance system can require.

I learned that an NVR receives video feeds from IP/CCTV cameras and stores the recorded footage on internal HDDs. The basic relationship is:

**CCTV Cameras → Network/Switch → NVR → HDD Storage**

The cameras generate video, the NVR receives and manages the video streams, and the HDDs provide the physical storage where recordings are saved.

CCTV systems can consume significant storage because cameras may record continuously, sometimes 24 hours a day. The amount of storage required depends on factors such as:

* Number of cameras
* Camera resolution
* Recording quality
* Frame rate
* Whether recording is continuous or motion-based
* Number of days that recordings need to be retained

Multiple HDDs can therefore be installed in an NVR to provide more storage capacity and allow the system to retain recorded footage for longer periods. Surveillance systems commonly use HDDs designed for continuous workloads because the drives may be reading and writing data constantly.

### 2. Connecting the NVR for Configuration

After preparing the HDDs, I also connected the peripherals required to configure the NVR.

I connected:

* **Monitor** — to display the NVR's interface.
* **Mouse** — to navigate through the NVR configuration menus.
* **Keyboard** — for entering information and navigating/configuring the system.
* **Power cable** — to supply power to the NVR.
* **LAN/Ethernet cable** — to connect the NVR to the network.

The LAN connection is particularly important because the NVR needs network connectivity to communicate with IP cameras and other devices on the surveillance network. Network connectivity can also allow authorized users or systems to access and manage the NVR remotely, depending on the organization's configuration.

This gave me practical exposure to how a CCTV NVR is physically prepared before configuration and deployment. It showed me that configuring a surveillance recorder involves more than simply powering it on: the storage, display, input devices, power, and network connection all need to be properly connected.

### 3. Setting Up a PC at Another Location for Training

I also helped set up a **PC at a different location for a training session**.

During the setup, we encountered a network-related problem involving the trainer's laptop. The trainer's laptop belonged to a **different branch of the company**, meaning its configuration was different from the systems normally used at the location where the training was taking place.

The laptop did not have **Geniac** installed. Geniac appeared to be one of the software/configuration requirements used within the company's IT/network environment.

Because the required software was not present, the laptop could not function on the network in the expected way. This meant that the issue was not simply a faulty Ethernet cable or network device; the computer itself was missing part of the configuration required for network access.

### 4. Administrator Access Problem

We considered installing Geniac on the trainer's laptop to resolve the issue. However, another problem was discovered.

Installing software on the laptop required **administrator privileges**. The administrator account had previously been configured by another IT personnel, and the current team did not have the administrator password.

Therefore, even though the required software had been identified, installation could not proceed normally because the necessary administrative credentials were unavailable.

This demonstrated an important aspect of IT support: solving a technical problem can depend on having the correct **access privileges and credentials**. A technician may know exactly what software needs to be installed, but without authorized administrator access, the installation or configuration may not be possible.

### 5. Main Technical Lessons

The activities exposed me to several areas of practical IT:

**CCTV/NVR hardware**

* HDDs are used inside NVRs to store surveillance recordings.
* NVRs can require multiple HDDs because CCTV footage consumes large amounts of storage.
* HDD mounting components help secure the drives inside the recorder.
* An NVR requires power, display/input peripherals, and network connectivity for configuration.

**Networking**

* NVRs communicate with IP cameras through the network.
* Network connectivity involves more than simply plugging in an Ethernet cable.
* Corporate networks can have specific software and configuration requirements.
* Devices from different company branches may have different configurations.

**Software deployment**

* Required company software may need to be installed before a computer can properly access certain network resources.
* Installing software on a Windows computer can require administrator privileges.

**Access control**

* Administrator credentials are important for maintaining and configuring company computers.
* Missing administrator credentials can prevent authorized IT personnel from installing required software or making configuration changes.
* IT personnel therefore need proper credential management and access procedures.

### Overall Experience

Day 49 gave me exposure to two different areas of IT support: **CCTV infrastructure and corporate computer/network support**.

On the CCTV side, I physically prepared HDDs and connected the peripherals and LAN cable needed to configure a Hikvision NVR. This helped me understand the relationship between cameras, networking, NVRs, and storage.

On the computer-support side, I helped prepare a PC for training and encountered a real-world network configuration issue involving a laptop from another branch. The missing Geniac software and unavailable administrator password demonstrated how differences in system configuration and access privileges can complicate troubleshooting.

The day reinforced that IT troubleshooting is not always about replacing a faulty component. Problems can originate from **hardware, network configuration, software requirements, user/device configuration, or access permissions**, and identifying the actual cause is an important part of IT support.

# 9. PoE Splitter

**User asked:**

* “What of a poe splitter”

**Assistant explained that a PoE splitter (Power over Ethernet Splitter) would be a good technical diagram**, particularly for CCTV/networking work.

Suggested basic diagram:

```text
PoE Switch
     |
     | Ethernet Cable
     |
PoE Splitter
   /      \
Power    Data
  |         |
Device   Device
```

For CCTV specifically:

```text
PoE Switch
     |
Ethernet Cable
     |
PoE Splitter
   /      \
Power    Data
  |         |
Camera   Network
```

The assistant explained:

* A PoE splitter separates power and data coming through an Ethernet cable.
* It can be used when a device needs power and network/data separately.
* A CCTV camera can be shown as the endpoint.

The assistant compared two diagram choices:

### PoE Splitter Diagram

Best when:

* The user specifically learned about PoE.
* The user installed/handled PoE-related equipment.
* The user wants a more technical and less generic diagram.

### CCTV Network Diagram

Best when:

* The week was primarily about CCTV installation/configuration.
* The user wants the diagram to clearly represent their actual SIWES work.

# 13. Overall Diagram Strategy Developed

The key idea was to avoid putting random diagrams into the SIWES logbook.

Instead, each **5-day period should have one diagram that reflects the dominant technical activity of that week**.

Examples:

| Week/Days                              | Dominant Activity                  | Recommended Diagram                    |
| -------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------------- |
| Week 1 / Days 1–5                      | System configuration, errors, BSOD | **Computer Troubleshooting Flowchart** |
| Week containing strong switch activity | Switches/networking                | **Network Switch / Star Topology**     |
| Week containing CCTV installation      | CCTV + Ethernet                    | **CCTV Network Diagram**               |
| Week containing PoE work               | PoE equipment                      | **PoE Splitter Diagram**               |
| Week containing structured cabling     | Network outlets                    | **RJ45 Face Plate Diagram**            |
| Week containing desktop hardware setup | Computer hardware                  | **System Unit Internal Components**    |
| Week containing UPS installation       | Power protection                   | **UPS Connection Diagram**             |
| Week containing printer maintenance    | Printer hardware                   | **Printer Components Diagram**         |

The user specifically wants the diagrams to be **based on actual SIWES activities**, not merely generic IT diagrams.

A major requirement is therefore to check the actual five-day activities before assigning a diagram, especially where the user remembers discussing a particular component such as a **switch, PoE splitter, or face plate**.

---

## Current Best-Known Recommendations

### Week 1

**Computer Troubleshooting Flowchart**
Because the user clarified that the first week involved system configuration, fixing errors, and Blue Screen of Death troubleshooting.

### Week 10 (Days 46–50)

**CCTV Network Diagram**
Because the period included significant CCTV/Ethernet/switch activity, as well as desktop setup and UPS work.

### Hardware-focused week

**Labeled System Unit/Laptop Internal Components Diagram**
For weeks where actual computer hardware was the dominant subject.

### PoE-focused week

**PoE Splitter Diagram**
Especially suitable if the actual day's activity involved a PoE splitter or camera power/data separation.

### Structured-cabling week

**RJ45 Face Plate Diagram**
Appropriate only if the week's activities actually involved network outlets/structured cabling or related office network infrastructure.

## Important Accuracy Note

Some earlier responses made associations between diagrams and days based on the SIWES information available in context rather than a complete day-by-day extraction of the entire project history. Therefore, those associations should be treated as **working recommendations, not definitive records**, unless the original SIWES entry confirms them.

* Despite the outage, important IT infrastructure continued operating.
* The **network**, **Navision**, and **Synology** services remained functional.
* This was possible because the server room had a backup power system/CPU capable of keeping the critical infrastructure powered for approximately **six hours**.
* The backup power supply allowed critical systems and services to remain available during the prolonged power interruption.
* This demonstrated the importance of backup power in maintaining business-critical IT infrastructure.

**Systems/services observed remaining operational:**

* Network infrastructure.
* Navision.
* Synology.

**Skills/Knowledge gained:**

* Observed the effect of power outages on computer systems and network infrastructure.

DAY 53
### SIWES Day 53 — Complete Summary

On SIWES Day 53, I participated in and observed several IT-related activities involving workstation relocation, wireless networking, CCTV/NVR configuration, and planning for network infrastructure in a new office.

#### 1. Installation of Two Wireless Access Points

I followed Mr. Y to install **two wireless access points (APs)**. I observed and assisted with the practical installation of the devices as part of the wireless network infrastructure.

This activity exposed me to the deployment of wireless networking equipment and helped me understand that access points are installed to provide or improve wireless network coverage and connectivity within an area.

#### 2. Relocation of a Staff Desktop Computer

I followed Mr. Y to **reposition a staff member's desktop computer to the conference room** so the staff member could carry out work from that location.

The computer was moved from its previous position and set up in the conference room. The necessary components and connections had to be restored after the relocation so the workstation could be used properly.

This gave me practical exposure to **workstation relocation and setup**, as well as the need to handle computer equipment carefully when moving it between locations.

#### 3. Observation of NVR Configuration and CCTV Cameras

I observed the **configuration of a Network Video Recorder (NVR) with a monitor**. Following the configuration, the connected CCTV cameras were successfully working and their video feeds could be displayed through the NVR and monitor.

This helped me understand the relationship between **CCTV cameras, an NVR, and a monitoring display**. The NVR serves as a central device for managing and recording the feeds from connected cameras, while the monitor allows the camera feeds to be viewed.

The activity also showed me that after connecting cameras to the appropriate system, the NVR must be properly configured before the camera feeds can be viewed and managed effectively.

#### 4. Visit to a New Office and Network Planning

I followed Mr. Y to a **new office** where I learned an important principle about planning network infrastructure.

I learned that the **networking structure should be planned after the office's tables, chairs, and seating arrangements have been determined and finalized**.

The physical arrangement of the office affects decisions such as:

* Where network points should be located.
* Where computers and other network devices will be positioned.
* How Ethernet cables should be routed.
* Where access points may need to be installed.
* How cable lengths and routes can be minimized.
* How the network can be installed without interfering with the office's workspace.

This demonstrated that network installation is not only about connecting devices but also requires consideration of the **physical environment and workspace layout**.

### Key Technical Lessons from Day 53

* Learned about the installation and deployment of **wireless access points**.
* Observed practical installation of **two access points**.
* Gained experience with **relocating and setting up a desktop workstation**.
* Observed the configuration of an **NVR and monitor** for CCTV surveillance.
* Learned that CCTV cameras need proper NVR configuration before their feeds can be effectively viewed and managed.
* Learned the importance of considering the **physical office layout before designing network infrastructure**.
* Understood that tables, chairs, and staff seating positions can influence the placement of network points, cables, computers, and wireless devices.
* Gained further exposure to the practical relationship between **IT infrastructure and office planning**.
* Learned that proper planning before installation can make network deployment more organized, efficient, and suitable for the actual workspace.

### Overall Day 53 Experience

Day 53 provided practical exposure to several areas of IT support and infrastructure. The activities covered **wireless networking, workstation deployment, CCTV surveillance systems, and network infrastructure planning**. A major lesson from the day was that successful IT installation depends not only on technical configuration but also on understanding the physical environment in which the technology will be deployed.



DAY 54
Here is an extensive summary of everything covered for **SIWES Day 54**, including your original observations and the technical details I added:

---

## SIWES Day 54 — Complete Summary

### 1. Projector and Canon Plotter Maintenance

* I observed the **maintenance of a projector**.
* I also observed the **maintenance of a Canon plotter**.
* I was assigned responsibility for **checking the Canon plotter weekly** going forward.
* The purpose of the regular checks is to help ensure that the plotter remains functional and to identify possible problems early.
* This gave me practical exposure to **routine/preventive maintenance of office equipment**, particularly equipment used for printing and large-format output.

### 2. Repair of a Damaged CCTV Camera Network Cable

* I accompanied **Mr. F** to work on a CCTV camera whose **network cable had been accidentally cut by the electrical team**.
* Since the cable was damaged, the camera's network connection needed to be restored.
* Mr. F temporarily joined the damaged sections of the cable together so that the connection could work again.
* I learned that this was only a **temporary repair**.
* The recommended **long-term solution** is to run a new cable/travel through the appropriate route and use it to replace the damaged section/cable.
* The damaged cable would then be pulled out/replaced rather than relying permanently on the temporary joint.

### 3. Tools Used During Cable Repair

During the repair, I learned the differences between several hand tools and their individual purposes:

**Wire stripper**

* Used to remove the protective insulation/coating around a copper conductor.
* This exposes the copper wire so that the conductors can be properly joined or terminated.
* The purpose is to remove the insulation without unnecessarily damaging the copper conductor.

**Wire cutter**

* Used to cut network/electrical wires and cables to the required length.
* It is specifically designed for cutting rather than removing insulation.

**Pliers**

* Used to grip, press, bend, and twist wires.
* In this repair, they were useful for manipulating and joining the copper conductors.

**Insulation tape**

* Used to cover and protect the exposed/repaired section of the cable.
* It provides insulation and helps protect the repaired area from accidental contact and damage.

### 4. Cable Management and Protection

* I learned that repairing or installing a network cable is not only about establishing connectivity.
* **Proper cable management is important after laying cables.**
* Cables should be properly routed, secured, and concealed/protected where necessary.
* Poorly positioned or exposed cables can easily be damaged by other workers, equipment, movement, or maintenance activities.
* The CCTV cable had been accidentally cut by another team, demonstrating why network cables need to be properly protected and routed.
* Proper cable routing also helps reduce the likelihood of future faults and makes maintenance easier.

### 5. Technical Lessons From the Day

Day 54 exposed me to several practical IT infrastructure and maintenance concepts:

* **Preventive maintenance** of office equipment.
* Routine inspection of a **Canon plotter**.
* Basic maintenance of a **projector**.
* Troubleshooting a CCTV camera connectivity problem caused by a damaged network cable.
* Temporary restoration of a damaged network connection.
* Difference between a **temporary cable repair and a permanent cable replacement**.
* Proper handling of copper conductors.
* Identification and use of **wire strippers, wire cutters, and pliers**.
* Use of **insulation tape** when protecting repaired wires.
* Importance of proper **network cable routing and cable management**.
* Importance of protecting network infrastructure from accidental physical damage.

### 6. Overall Practical Experience

The day combined **office equipment maintenance with network infrastructure work**. I gained experience observing the maintenance of a projector and Canon plotter, while also participating in a practical CCTV/network cable repair with Mr. F. The cable incident helped me understand that network reliability depends not only on configuration and connectivity but also on the **physical installation and protection of network infrastructure**.

A key lesson from the day was that a temporary repair can restore functionality, but a **proper permanent solution should be implemented to ensure reliability and prevent the same problem from recurring**.

DAY 61

**SIWES Day 61**

### Printer and Ink Cartridge Maintenance

* Learned about **printers**, particularly inkjet/colour printers, and how their ink cartridges are replaced.
* Observed a **colour printer** that uses four separate ink cartridges based on the **CMYK colour model**:

  * **C — Cyan**
  * **M — Magenta**
  * **Y — Yellow**
  * **K — Black**
* The cartridges were physically similar in appearance to small batteries and had **three gold-coloured contact points/contacts** used for electrical communication between the cartridge and the printer.
* The **cyan cartridge** was replaced because the printer was having an issue with it.
* The replacement cartridge was **new**.
* The **protective tape/seal had already been removed** from the new cartridge.
* The **cartridge contacts were cleaned** as part of troubleshooting.
* Despite using a new cartridge and cleaning the contacts, the printer still **failed to recognize/process the cyan ink cartridge**.
* Because the cartridge was new, the protective material had been removed, the contacts had been cleaned, and the problem persisted, it was concluded that the **print head was probably faulty**.
* Other possible causes of cartridge-recognition problems were discussed generally, including damaged contacts/connectors or an electronic/main-board fault, but in this particular case the print head was considered the likely fault based on the troubleshooting already performed.

### Understanding Printer Error Conditions

* I wondered whether the printer could simply continue printing even if it refused to recognize the new cyan cartridge.
* It was clarified that **many printers will not allow printing while a critical cartridge, ink-detection, or print-head error remains unresolved**.
* Therefore, even though the new cartridge physically contains ink and is installed in the printer, the printer's internal system may prevent printing when it detects an error.
* Depending on the printer model, an error involving one colour cartridge can potentially prevent the printer from printing altogether, rather than allowing it to print using only the remaining colours or black.
* The important lesson was that **having ink physically present is not necessarily enough for a printer to print**. The printer must also successfully recognize the cartridge and consider the relevant components to be functioning correctly.

### Computer BIOS Information and Asset Identification

* Also worked with several PCs and learned how to retrieve hardware identification information using the **Windows Management Instrumentation Command-line (WMIC)** utility.
* Used WMIC to obtain the:

  * **BIOS serial number**
  * **Computer manufacturer**
* The command used was:

```cmd
wmic bios get serialnumber,manufacturer
```

* The command retrieves information stored in the computer's BIOS and displays the serial number and manufacturer.
* This is useful during IT maintenance and inventory work because computers can be identified and documented using their unique serial numbers.
* BIOS serial numbers can be useful for **asset tracking, hardware inventory, maintenance records, and identifying individual PCs**.

### Main Technical Lessons from Day 61

1. Learned how **colour printer cartridges** work and how they are replaced.
2. Learned the **CMYK colour system** used by colour printers: Cyan, Magenta, Yellow, and Black.
3. Learned that cartridges communicate with the printer through **electrical contacts**.
4. Learned basic troubleshooting for a cartridge that is not recognized:

   * Confirm the cartridge is new.
   * Remove the protective tape/seal.
   * Ensure it is correctly installed.
   * Clean the cartridge contacts.
   * If the problem persists, investigate the print head or other printer hardware.
5. Learned that a printer may **refuse to print when it detects a cartridge or print-head error**, even when ink is physically present.
6. Learned how to use **WMIC** to retrieve BIOS information from PCs.
7. Learned that **BIOS serial numbers and manufacturer information** can be used for computer identification and inventory documentation.
8. Gained practical exposure to both **printer troubleshooting/maintenance** and **PC hardware identification** during SIWES.

### 3. Scanner Troubleshooting

* The issue was found to be minor: the **power cable had been disconnected/removed**.
* Reconnecting the power cable resolved the issue.
* This reinforced the importance of checking simple physical connections before assuming that a device has a technical fault.

### 4. CCTV Camera Troubleshooting

* Followed **Mr. F** to investigate several **offline CCTV cameras**.
* The cameras were initially identified as being offline and required troubleshooting.
* During the investigation, the problem was traced to a **damaged/faulty network switch**.
* The affected cameras depended on the switch for network connectivity, so the switch failure caused the cameras connected through it to go offline.
* This provided practical exposure to diagnosing CCTV problems from the network infrastructure side rather than immediately treating the cameras themselves as faulty.

## Technical Concepts Encountered

* **Ping testing:** Used to check whether network devices are reachable.
* **Network switches:** Understanding their role in connecting network devices and CCTV equipment.
* **Basic network troubleshooting:** Checking connectivity and identifying where a failure originates.
* **Physical troubleshooting:** Checking power connections before performing more complex diagnostics.
* **CCTV networking:** Understanding that cameras can go offline because of network infrastructure problems such as a damaged switch.
* **Fault isolation:** Determining whether the problem is with the end device, power supply, cable, or shared network equipment.

## Key Lessons Learned

* Always check simple physical causes, such as disconnected power cables, before assuming a device has a serious fault.
* A single faulty network switch can cause multiple connected devices, including CCTV cameras, to become unreachable.
* Ping is a useful first-level tool for checking network connectivity.
* Troubleshooting should proceed systematically by identifying where communication or power is being interrupted.
* Network problems affecting multiple devices may originate from a shared infrastructure component rather than from each individual device.

DAY 67
## SIWES Day 67 — Detailed Summary

### Activities Performed

**1. Plotter Maintenance**

* Carried out routine maintenance on the plotter as part of the Learning Center/IT equipment maintenance activities.

**2. Biometric Device Network Troubleshooting**

* Checked a biometric device that was experiencing a network connectivity problem.
* The issue was traced to a **PoE (Power over Ethernet) switch** that was having a power-related problem.
* The switch apparently did not have a dedicated power button.
* To restart/reboot the switch, the wall socket supplying power to it was switched off and then switched back on.
* After power-cycling the switch, the network issue was resolved and the biometric device was restored to normal operation.
* Learned that some network switches may not have a physical power button, meaning that restarting them may require disconnecting and reconnecting their power supply.

**3. Desktop Network Issue**

* Troubleshot a desktop computer that was experiencing a network connectivity problem.
* The desktop was receiving its network connection through an **Avaya IP phone**.
* The Avaya device was experiencing a **PoE/power issue** because its power adapter had become loose.
* Since the desktop's network connection depended on the Avaya device, the loose adapter caused the desktop to lose its network connection.
* The issue was resolved by properly adjusting/repositioning the power adapter so that the Avaya device received power correctly.
* The affected user was informed about the problem so they would be aware that the adapter/power connection was the underlying cause and could take appropriate action if it happened again.
* This demonstrated how a problem with an IP phone or intermediary network device can affect the network connection of a computer connected through it.

**4. CCTV Camera PoE Troubleshooting**

* Assisted with troubleshooting a CCTV camera that was experiencing a **PoE-related power/connectivity problem**.
* During troubleshooting, it was discovered that the particular camera was not compatible with the **PoE splitter** being considered/used.
* The appropriate solution for the camera was a **PoE injector**, which was compatible with the camera.
* Learned that different PoE equipment serves different purposes and that compatibility between the camera, switch, injector, and splitter needs to be considered when setting up or troubleshooting network cameras.

### Technical Knowledge Learned

**PoE (Power over Ethernet):**

* PoE allows electrical power and network/data communication to be carried through Ethernet cabling.
* PoE equipment can be used to provide power to devices such as IP cameras, biometric devices, and IP phones without requiring a separate conventional power connection at the device.

**PoE Switch:**

* A PoE switch can provide both network connectivity and electrical power to compatible network devices through Ethernet cables.
* If a PoE switch develops a power problem, connected devices may lose both power and network connectivity.

**PoE Injector:**

* A PoE injector is used to introduce electrical power into an Ethernet connection so that a compatible device can receive power through its network cable.
* In the camera troubleshooting exercise, the camera required a PoE injector rather than the PoE splitter that had initially been considered.
* The injector is generally positioned toward the network/source side of the connection, before the Ethernet cable carrying both power and data goes to the powered device.

**PoE Splitter:**

* A PoE splitter is used at the **device/camera end** of a connection.
* It separates the power and data received through a PoE Ethernet connection so that a non-PoE device can receive them through the appropriate connections.
* The exercise demonstrated that not every camera is compatible with every PoE arrangement, so the device's requirements must be checked before choosing a splitter or injector.

### Overall Learning

Day 67 involved practical troubleshooting of several network-related problems involving **PoE switches, biometric equipment, Avaya IP phones, desktop computers, CCTV cameras, PoE injectors, and PoE splitters**. The main lessons were that power-related faults can directly cause network connectivity problems, some switches must be power-cycled through their wall socket because they lack a power button, and PoE equipment must be selected according to the requirements and compatibility of the connected device.


DAY 68
## SIWES Day 68 — Detailed Summary

### Main Activity: Troubleshooting a Slow Windows Desktop

The day involved troubleshooting a Windows desktop computer that was experiencing **severe performance issues**. The computer reportedly took approximately **30 minutes to power on/boot**, and even after starting, it took a long time to open software and applications.

### Troubleshooting Process

1. **Verified the storage drive**

   * The first thing done was to confirm that the computer's original **HDD had been replaced with an SSD**.
   * This was checked because the system was unusually slow despite having an SSD installed.

2. **Checked the RAM**

   * The computer was checked to determine its available memory.
   * It was confirmed that the system had **8 GB of RAM**.

3. **Checked system uptime**

   * The system showed an uptime of approximately **6 days**.
   * This indicated that the computer had been running for several days without a proper restart.
   * The team considered this as a possible contributor to the system's poor performance.

4. **Checked Fast Startup**

   * The Windows Power Options were accessed using **`powercfg.cpl`** through the Windows Run/command interface.
   * The **Fast Startup** setting was checked to determine whether it was enabled or disabled.
   * The system was then properly restarted rather than simply continuing to run.

5. **Monitored CPU usage**

   * After the proper reboot, the system's performance was observed.
   * The **CPU usage was close to 100% even though no obvious application was actively being used**.
   * This suggested that background processes or accumulated system files might be contributing to the high CPU usage.

6. **Cleared temporary files**

   * As part of the troubleshooting and maintenance process, unnecessary temporary files were removed.
   * The following locations were cleared:

     * **Temp**
     * **%Temp%**
     * **Prefetch**
   * The files were permanently deleted as part of the cleanup process.

7. **Observed the system after cleanup**

   * After clearing the temporary files, the computer was monitored to see whether there was any change in performance and CPU activity.
   * The purpose was to determine whether the cleanup had helped reduce the unusually high CPU usage and improve system responsiveness.

### Technical Knowledge Gained

* Learned how to troubleshoot a **very slow Windows computer systematically** rather than immediately assuming the storage drive was the problem.
* Learned to verify whether an **HDD had been upgraded to an SSD** when investigating slow performance.
* Learned how to check a computer's **RAM capacity** and **system uptime**.
* Learned that a computer can remain running for several days and that a **proper restart** can be useful during troubleshooting.
* Learned how to access Windows **Power Options using `powercfg.cpl`** and check the Fast Startup configuration.
* Learned to use **Task Manager/system monitoring to identify unusually high CPU utilization**.
* Learned that unnecessary temporary files can be cleared from **Temp, `%Temp%`, and Prefetch** as part of Windows maintenance and troubleshooting.
* Gained practical experience in observing the computer **before and after maintenance** to determine whether the troubleshooting steps produced an improvement.

### Concise Overall Description

**Troubleshot a severely slow Windows desktop that took about 30 minutes to boot and was slow to launch applications. Verified its SSD upgrade and 8 GB RAM, checked its six-day uptime and Fast Startup settings using `powercfg.cpl`, performed a proper restart, and investigated nearly 100% CPU usage while idle. Cleared Temp, `%Temp%`, and Prefetch files and monitored the system afterward to assess changes in performance.**

# Windows + R, IP Addresses, and Folders

### User's Question

The user then asked about the "mystery" behind entering an IP address through **Windows + R** and having it lead to a folder.

The specific concept being referred to is entering a network path such as:

```text
\\192.168.1.100
```

into the Windows Run dialog.

### Explanation Given

The assistant explained that what appears to be a folder is usually a **network share**, rather than a normal folder stored on the user's own computer or a public folder on the internet.

Windows can access shared files and folders on another computer or server over a network using the **SMB (Server Message Block)** protocol.

For example:

```text
\\192.168.1.20
```

could point to a computer or server with the IP address `192.168.1.20`.

If that device has file sharing enabled and the user has permission to access it, Windows File Explorer can display the shared folders.

The folders might contain things such as:

* Shared Documents
* Software
* Public files
* Backups
* Company resources

Depending on permissions, the user may be able to open files, copy files, save files, or otherwise interact with the shared resources.

### Why Windows Treats It Like a Folder

The important idea is that **SMB makes remote resources appear in Windows similarly to local filesystem resources**.

The computer does not necessarily download the entire folder to the user's PC. Instead, Windows communicates with the remote computer/server over the network and presents the available shared resources through File Explorer.

The user can therefore interact with a remote shared folder almost as though it were a folder on their own computer.

### Example Network

An example was given:

```text
User's PC:     192.168.1.10
File Server:   192.168.1.20
```

Entering:

```text
\\192.168.1.20
```

into Windows + R can cause File Explorer to show the shares available on the server.

### Why It Does Not Work With Every IP Address

Simply entering an IP address does not automatically produce a folder.

For the network-share method to work, the target device generally needs to:

1. Be reachable from the user's computer.
2. Have network/file sharing enabled.
3. Have SMB services available.
4. Have one or more shared folders/resources.
5. Allow the user's account to access those resources through its permissions/firewall/security configuration.

If those conditions aren't met, Windows may display an error such as:

```text
Windows cannot access \\192.168.1.20
```

### Difference Between an IP Address in a Browser and a Windows Network Path

The assistant highlighted an important distinction.

#### Web/HTTP access

Entering:

```text
http://192.168.1.20
```

in a web browser means the user is attempting to communicate with a **web server/web interface** on that device.

For example, it could open:

* A router administration page
* A printer's web configuration interface
* A server's web application
* A network device's management interface

#### Windows/SMB file sharing

Entering:

```text
\\192.168.1.20
```

into Windows Run or File Explorer means the user is attempting to access **Windows/network file shares**, normally through SMB.

So the two forms may use the same IP address but represent completely different services:

```text
http://192.168.1.20
        ↓
     Web service

\\192.168.1.20
        ↓
   SMB file sharing
        ↓
   Shared folders
```

### SIWES Relevance

The assistant connected the concept to the user's SIWES environment.

In a company network, a central server may contain shared resources such as:

* Software installers
* Printer drivers
* Documents
* Reports
* Shared departmental files
* Backup resources

Instead of physically copying files from the server to every employee's PC, users can access the appropriate shared folder over the network.

This is particularly relevant to the user's SIWES experience because they have been working around **servers, switches, printers/plotters, CCTV systems, network infrastructure, Active Directory, and other IT resources**.

### Key Concepts to Remember

**IP address**

* Identifies a device/interface on an IP network.
* Example: `192.168.1.20`.

**SMB**

* A network protocol commonly used for file and printer sharing.
* Allows computers to access shared resources across a network.

**Network share**

* A folder/resource that a computer or server makes available to other authorized computers on the network.

**UNC path**

* The `\\server\share` format used by Windows to identify a network resource.
* Example:

```text
\\192.168.1.20\Shared
```

or:

```text
\\FileServer\Shared
```

**Windows + R**

* Opens the Windows Run dialog.
* It can be used to enter a UNC network path and access shared resources.

### Most Important Takeaway

The "mystery" is essentially:

```text
IP address
   ↓
Remote computer/server
   ↓
SMB file-sharing service
   ↓
Shared folder
   ↓
Windows File Explorer displays it like a folder
```

So when a user types something like:

```text
\\192.168.1.20
```

and Windows opens a folder-like view, they are **browsing shared resources on another computer/server over the network**, not magically opening a folder belonging to the IP address itself.

### Technical Concepts Encountered

* **Network Switch:** A networking device used to connect devices within a LAN and forward Ethernet traffic.
* **Switch Management:** The process of accessing and configuring a switch through its management interface.
* **Management Network/Management VLAN:** A dedicated network or VLAN through which network equipment can be administered.
* **Trunking:** A switch-port configuration that allows traffic belonging to multiple VLANs to pass through a single link.
* **LAN/Ethernet Cable:** Used to establish the physical network connection between the laptop and switch during the configuration.
* **PoE (Power over Ethernet):** Technology that allows compatible network devices such as CCTV cameras to receive electrical power through Ethernet cabling.
* **PoE Splitter:** A device used to separate power and network/data from a PoE connection for compatible equipment.
* **CCTV Network Cabling:** The Ethernet cable provides the network/data connection required for IP-based CCTV cameras and can also carry PoE when the setup supports it.

### Practical Lessons Learned

* Before attempting to manage a switch, the administrator's laptop must have appropriate network connectivity to the switch's management interface.
* Physical Ethernet connectivity is important when initially accessing/configuring network equipment.
* Switches can be configured with management settings and trunk ports rather than remaining in their default configuration.
* A CCTV troubleshooting problem is not always caused by the camera or PoE equipment; the **network cable itself can be the source of the fault**.
* Replacing a faulty cable can restore connectivity when the camera, PoE equipment, and other components are functioning properly.
* CCTV maintenance can require physical installation work at height in addition to network troubleshooting.
* The day provided practical exposure to both **network administration** and **CCTV infrastructure maintenance**.

I also gained my first practical experience working at height on a CCTV camera and using a PoE splitter during troubleshooting. After investigating the camera issue, the problem was identified as a faulty network cable, and the solution was to install/run a new cable.

DAY 77
## SIWES Day 77 — Comprehensive Summary

### 1. What happened

I had my first experience observing a **LAN cable rerunning**.

A network problem was discovered in an office. The LAN cable providing the **uplink to the office switch** appeared to have a problem. Because this cable was responsible for connecting the office switch to the wider network, the entire office lost network connectivity.

The problem affected both:

* PCs in the office
* Avaya IP phones

This showed that the problem was not limited to one computer or one device. The common connection—the switch uplink—was suspected.

### 2. Troubleshooting the LAN cable

During troubleshooting, I learned a lot about how a **LAN cable tester** works and how to use one.

The team repeatedly:

* Removed/recrimped the RJ45 connectors.
* Tested the cable using LAN testers at both ends.
* Checked whether the individual conductors had continuity.
* Tried different ways of terminating the cable.
* Tried different approaches to avoid having to rerun the entire cable.

The cable was recrimped multiple times, and the LAN tester was used after the attempts to determine whether the cable connections were correct.

### 3. Attempt to avoid rerunning the cable

Because rerunning the cable would require physically running a new cable through the existing route, several attempts were made to salvage the existing cable.

One of the ideas was to change the **wire/color arrangement** so that a suspected damaged conductor could potentially be avoided.

This made me realize that cable troubleshooting is not always immediately about replacing the cable. Technicians may first try to identify whether the fault is at the connector/termination or somewhere along the cable itself.

However, we later discussed that changing individual wire assignments is **not normally a proper permanent solution**, especially if it breaks the normal twisted-pair arrangement.

### 4. The 1-2-3-6 pins

Another thing attempted was using the conductors corresponding to pins:

**1, 2, 3 and 6**

These are the four conductors used by **10BASE-T and 100BASE-TX Ethernet** because these standards use two twisted pairs:

* Pins 1 & 2 → one pair
* Pins 3 & 6 → another pair

The wires associated with these pins are normally from the **orange and green pairs** under the T568B wiring standard.

The idea was essentially to see whether the working pairs could be used to establish network connectivity while avoiding a potentially damaged pair.

The LAN tester showed continuity, but the network still did not work.

### 5. Important discovery: continuity does not necessarily mean the cable is good

One of the main things I learned from the troubleshooting was that a cable can **pass a basic continuity test and still fail to establish an Ethernet link**.

A basic LAN tester mainly checks things such as:

* Whether conductors are connected from one end to the other.
* Whether there is an open circuit.
* Whether there is a short/crossed connection.
* Whether the conductors appear in the expected order.

However, a simple tester does not necessarily verify the cable's ability to reliably carry Ethernet signals at the required electrical characteristics and speed.

Therefore:

**LAN tester says continuity → does NOT automatically mean the cable is healthy for Ethernet.**

A cable could have physical damage, poor electrical characteristics, excessive resistance, damaged twisting, or other problems that prevent an Ethernet interface from establishing a link.

### 6. The importance of the switch link light

I later clarified that there was **no link light at all** on the switch port.

This was an important piece of information.

A link/activity LED on an Ethernet switch port generally indicates that the physical Ethernet connection has been detected.

Since there was **no link light**, the problem was most likely at **Layer 1 — the Physical Layer**.

That means the troubleshooting should focus first on things such as:

* LAN cable
* RJ45 connectors
* Cable termination
* Switch port
* Network interface/physical connection

rather than immediately investigating things such as IP addresses, DHCP, DNS, VLANs, or routing.

### 7. Why the cable could pass the tester but produce no link

The most likely explanation is that the cable had some form of physical/electrical damage.

Possible examples include:

* A conductor being partially broken.
* A cable being crushed or excessively bent.
* Damage somewhere along the cable run.
* Poor-quality cable.
* Excessive resistance.
* Damage that affected the cable's ability to carry Ethernet signals.
* Damage to the twisted-pair characteristics.

A basic continuity tester may still detect an electrical path through a damaged conductor, while the Ethernet interfaces on the switch and connected equipment require the signal to meet much stricter requirements before they establish a link.

Therefore, the LAN tester and the switch were effectively testing **different things**.

**LAN tester:** "Is there electrical continuity between these points?"

**Ethernet switch:** "Can I establish a valid Ethernet physical link with the device/cable on the other end?"

### 8. Why changing the color codes was questionable

We discussed that changing the individual wire/color arrangement is not normally a good permanent solution.

Ethernet cables use **twisted pairs** for an important reason: the twisting helps reduce electromagnetic interference and crosstalk.

If individual wires are rearranged without preserving the proper twisted pairs, a continuity tester may still show that the wires are connected, but Ethernet performance can suffer.

Also, both ends would have to be terminated consistently for the pin assignments to make sense.

So while the attempt was understandable as a troubleshooting experiment, it should not be treated as the normal way to repair a damaged Ethernet cable.

### 9. Why using pins 1, 2, 3 and 6 didn't solve the problem

The attempt to use pins 1, 2, 3 and 6 was based on the fact that 10/100 Mbps Ethernet only requires two pairs.

However, simply having continuity on those four pins does not guarantee that the cable can establish an Ethernet link.

If the cable had a deeper physical problem, changing which conductors were used would not necessarily solve it.

Also, if the cable had already been damaged along its route, changing the termination at the ends would not repair the damaged section in the middle.

### 10. The final solution

After several troubleshooting attempts, the decision was made to **rerun the LAN cable**.

A completely new cable was installed through another/appropriate route and connected to the relevant equipment.

The network connection returned successfully.

This strongly indicated that the original cable itself was faulty and likely **damaged somewhere along its length and effectively unsalvageable**.

The fact that the new cable restored connectivity was strong practical evidence that the original cable was the problem.

### 11. What I learned technically

This experience taught me several practical networking concepts:

#### LAN uplink

An uplink connects an office/access switch to another network device or the wider network.

If the uplink fails, multiple devices connected to that switch can lose network access simultaneously.

#### LAN cable tester

A basic LAN tester can help identify:

* Open conductors
* Incorrect wiring order
* Shorts/crossed connections
* Continuity problems

However, it is not necessarily a complete certification test of Ethernet cable performance.

#### RJ45 termination

A poor RJ45 termination can prevent a network connection from working. Recrimping is therefore one of the first things that can be attempted when troubleshooting a suspected cable/connector problem.

#### Ethernet link light

The absence of a link light is an important clue.

**No link light → investigate the physical connection first.**

### 12. The troubleshooting logic from this experience

The troubleshooting process can be understood roughly as:

**Office devices lose network → check common connection → suspect switch/uplink → inspect cable → test connectors/cable → recrimp → test again → try to salvage cable → still no link → rerun cable → link restored.**

The key distinction is:

**Physical connection problem (Layer 1)**
vs.
**Network/configuration problem (Layers 2/3 and above).**

Because there was no link light, the problem was most consistent with a physical-layer fault.

### 13. Possible causes of the original cable failure

We cannot know the exact cause without examining the removed cable, but possibilities include:

* Physical damage somewhere along the route.
* Excessive bending.
* Crushing or pressure on the cable.
* A partially broken conductor.
* Damaged twisted pairs.
* Poor-quality cable.
* Environmental deterioration.
* A bad connector/termination that could not be resolved through recrimping.

Because the cable was repeatedly recrimped and eventually replaced successfully, damage somewhere along the cable run became the most likely explanation.

### 14. Main practical lesson

The biggest lesson from Day 77 was that **network troubleshooting involves separating a problem into layers and testing each possibility rather than assuming that one test proves everything is fine.**

In this case:

* The LAN tester showed continuity.
* The switch still had no link light.
* Recrimping did not solve the problem.
* Attempts to use alternative conductors did not solve it.
* A completely new cable immediately restored the connection.

Therefore, the original cable was most likely physically/electrically faulty despite appearing to have continuity.

### 15. SIWES knowledge gained

This day gave me practical exposure to:

* LAN cable troubleshooting
* RJ45 crimping
* LAN cable testing
* Understanding Ethernet pin assignments
* Understanding the purpose of twisted pairs
* Understanding 10/100 Mbps Ethernet pin usage
* Identifying physical-layer network faults
* Understanding uplinks between switches
* Using switch link LEDs as troubleshooting indicators
* Differentiating cable continuity from actual Ethernet link establishment
* Knowing when a cable should be replaced rather than continually repaired
* Real-world network troubleshooting and fault isolation

### Overall experience

Day 77 was particularly useful because it showed me that real-world networking problems are not always solved simply by following what a tester says. A cable may appear electrically continuous on a basic tester but still fail when an actual network device attempts to establish a link.

The experience also showed the importance of systematic troubleshooting: checking the obvious physical causes first, attempting reasonable repairs, testing after every change, and eventually replacing the faulty component when troubleshooting confirms that it cannot be reliably salvaged.


DAY 78
Here is an extensive combined summary of **everything you said and everything I added for SIWES Day 78**, preserving the technical details and the reasoning behind the incident:

### SIWES Day 78 — CCTV Switch/Uplink Troubleshooting

* I accompanied **Mr. F** to troubleshoot a problem involving a **CCTV/network switch**.
* The issue affected **three CCTV cameras**, which were offline/down.
* The three cameras were connected through a switch, but the switch itself was **not receiving network connectivity**.
* The problem was not initially with the cameras themselves; the issue was with the **network connection going into the switch**.
* The affected switch was receiving its network connection from another **upstream switch**.
* The connection between the affected switch and the upstream switch was made using a **patch cord**.
* During troubleshooting, it was discovered that the **patch cord was faulty**.
* Because the patch cord was faulty, the affected switch could not receive network connectivity from the upstream switch.
* Since the switch had no network connection, the three cameras connected through it could not communicate with the rest of the CCTV/network infrastructure and therefore appeared **offline**.
* This demonstrated that a problem with a single network component can affect multiple devices connected downstream from it.
* Replacing/fixing the faulty patch cord restored connectivity to the switch.
* Once the switch regained network connectivity, the **three CCTV cameras came back online**.
* The incident demonstrated the importance of checking the **uplink connection** when multiple network devices connected to the same switch go offline at the same time.
* I learned that when several IP cameras suddenly become unavailable simultaneously, the problem may be at the **switch, uplink, patch cord, or upstream network connection**, rather than with each individual camera.
* I also learned the importance of **systematic network troubleshooting**: instead of immediately replacing or inspecting the cameras themselves, the network path supplying them should be checked.
* The situation reinforced the concept of **upstream and downstream network devices**: the upstream switch provided connectivity to the affected switch, while the affected switch provided connectivity to the cameras.
* A faulty physical connection such as a patch cord can therefore cause a **network outage for several devices at once**.
* This was another practical experience involving **CCTV networking, Ethernet connections, switches, patch cords, network troubleshooting, and fault isolation**.

**Technical chain of the problem:**

`Upstream Switch → Faulty Patch Cord → CCTV/PoE Switch → 3 Cameras`

Because the patch cord was faulty:

`Upstream Switch ✖ → CCTV/PoE Switch → 3 Cameras ✖`

After correcting the faulty patch cord:

`Upstream Switch ✓ → CCTV/PoE Switch ✓ → 3 Cameras ✓`

**Main lesson:**
A network fault affecting several devices connected to the same switch can originate from the switch's **uplink connection**. Checking the physical link and patch cord between switches is an important troubleshooting step before assuming that the individual devices are faulty.

DAY 83
## SIWES Day 83 — Comprehensive Summary

### 1. What I originally did

On SIWES Day 83, I learned about **fibre optic patch cords**, specifically:

* **LC-to-LC fibre patch cords**
* **LC-to-SC fibre patch cords**

I learned about these different connector combinations and their use in fibre networking.

I was also assigned a **networking laboratory exercise**. The lab involved creating and configuring a network with:

* PCs
* Switches
* A router
* Two separate IP networks/subnets
* DHCP

The two networks were:

* **Administration:** `192.168.30.0/25`
* **Accounts:** `192.168.30.128/25`

There were two PCs for Administration and two PCs for Accounts.

I successfully completed the lab and learned a lot about:

* Router configuration
* Switch configuration
* IP addressing and subnetting
* DHCP
* Creating DHCP pools
* Connecting multiple network devices
* Testing network connectivity

### 2. Important correction about the topology

Initially, the lab was described as having one switch, but I later clarified that there were actually **two switches**.

I also clarified that I **did not configure VLANs** because I had not learned VLANs yet.

The router was connected **separately to both switches**, rather than having one switch connected to the other.

Therefore, the likely physical topology was:

```text
                  Router
                 /      \
                /        \
           Switch 1    Switch 2
           /      \      /      \
         PC1      PC2  PC3      PC4
       Admin     Admin Accounts Accounts
```

This means the two departments were physically separated through the two switches rather than logically separated using VLANs.

### 3. Likely IP addressing arrangement

The two `/25` networks divide the `192.168.30.0/24` address space into two separate subnets.

#### Administration

Network:

`192.168.30.0/25`

Likely router/default gateway:

`192.168.30.1`

Usable host range:

`192.168.30.1 – 192.168.30.126`

Broadcast:

`192.168.30.127`

Two Administration PCs could therefore receive addresses from this subnet through DHCP.

#### Accounts

Network:

`192.168.30.128/25`

Likely router/default gateway:

`192.168.30.129`

Usable host range:

`192.168.30.129 – 192.168.30.254`

Broadcast:

`192.168.30.255`

Two Accounts PCs could receive addresses from this subnet through DHCP.

### 4. Likely router configuration process

Because the router was connected separately to both switches and VLANs were not being used, the router likely had two physical interfaces involved.

For example:

```text
Router interface 1 → Administration switch
Router interface 2 → Accounts switch
```

The first router interface would belong to:

`192.168.30.0/25`

with a likely gateway of:

`192.168.30.1`

The second router interface would belong to:

`192.168.30.128/25`

with a likely gateway of:

`192.168.30.129`

The interfaces would need to be enabled using `no shutdown`.

### 5. Likely DHCP configuration

One of the major things I learned from the lab was **DHCP pool configuration**.

The router could be configured with two separate DHCP pools:

#### Administration DHCP pool

Network:

`192.168.30.0/25`

Default gateway:

`192.168.30.1`

This pool would automatically provide IP addresses to the two Administration PCs.

#### Accounts DHCP pool

Network:

`192.168.30.128/25`

Default gateway:

`192.168.30.129`

This pool would automatically provide IP addresses to the two Accounts PCs.

Gateway addresses would normally be excluded from the DHCP allocation so that they remain assigned to the router.

### 6. Likely PC configuration

The four PCs would be configured to obtain their IP addresses automatically through DHCP.

After the router's DHCP pools were configured, the PCs should receive addresses belonging to the correct subnet.

For example:

```text
Administration PC 1 → 192.168.30.x
Administration PC 2 → 192.168.30.x

Accounts PC 1 → 192.168.30.1xx
Accounts PC 2 → 192.168.30.1xx
```

The exact host addresses would depend on the DHCP configuration.

### 7. Likely testing process

After configuring the router, switches, DHCP, and PCs, the network would be tested.

Possible checks included:

* Checking that each PC received an IP address.
* Checking the subnet mask.
* Checking the default gateway.
* Pinging the router/default gateway.
* Testing communication between PCs.
* Confirming that the Administration PCs were using the `192.168.30.0/25` network.
* Confirming that the Accounts PCs were using the `192.168.30.128/25` network.
* Testing routing between the two networks.

### 8. What I probably learned from the switch configuration

Since I had not learned VLANs yet, the switches were probably being used in their basic/default switching role rather than being divided into VLANs.

The important concepts at this stage were likely:

* Connecting PCs to switch ports.
* Connecting switches to router interfaces.
* Understanding Ethernet connections.
* Understanding how a switch forwards traffic.
* Understanding how a router connects different IP networks.
* Understanding the difference between a switch and a router.
* Understanding how IP addresses are assigned to hosts.

There was therefore **no need to assume VLAN 10, VLAN 20, trunking, or Router-on-a-Stick** for this particular Day 83 lab.

### 9. Difference between this lab and a VLAN scenario

After clarifying the actual Day 83 setup, we discussed what would happen if I later encountered a **VLAN-based version** of the same scenario.

The important distinction is that the Day 83 lab used **physical separation**, while a VLAN scenario uses **logical separation**.

#### Day 83 approach

```text
                 Router
                /      \
               /        \
          Switch 1    Switch 2
           /    \      /    \
        Admin Admin  Accts Accts
```

Administration and Accounts were separated using different physical switch connections.

#### VLAN approach

With VLANs, the same departments could use one physical switch:

```text
                  Router
                    |
                 Trunk
                    |
                  Switch
            ┌───────┴───────┐
           VLAN 10         VLAN 20
          Admin PCs      Accounts PCs
```

For example:

* VLAN 10 → Administration
* VLAN 20 → Accounts

The switch ports connected to the Administration PCs would be assigned to VLAN 10.

The ports connected to the Accounts PCs would be assigned to VLAN 20.

The connection between the switch and router would be a **trunk** carrying traffic for both VLANs.

The router would then use **subinterfaces** to communicate with the different VLANs. This approach is commonly called **Router-on-a-Stick**.

### 10. Why VLANs are different

A VLAN allows one physical switch to behave as if it contains multiple separate logical networks.

Without VLANs:

* Physical devices/connections can be used to separate networks.
* Separate router interfaces can connect separate networks.

With VLANs:

* One switch can contain multiple logical networks.
* Switch ports can be assigned to different VLANs.
* A trunk can carry multiple VLANs.
* A router or Layer 3 switch can perform inter-VLAN routing.

### 11. Example of the VLAN version

A future version of the Day 83 exercise could look like:

```text
                 Router
                    |
                 Trunk
                    |
                 Switch
              /           \
          VLAN 10        VLAN 20
          Admin          Accounts
         PC1  PC2        PC3  PC4
```

The router could have:

* One subinterface for VLAN 10
* One subinterface for VLAN 20
* A separate IP network for each VLAN
* A DHCP pool for each network

For example:

```text
VLAN 10 — Administration
192.168.30.0/25

VLAN 20 — Accounts
192.168.30.128/25
```

The same two subnets from the Day 83 lab could therefore potentially be used in a VLAN exercise; the major difference would be how the networks are physically/logically separated and how the router connects to them.

### 12. Main technical concepts from Day 83

The important concepts associated with this day's work were:

**Networking hardware**

* Router
* Ethernet switch
* PCs
* Fibre optic patch cords

**Fibre**

* LC-to-LC patch cords
* LC-to-SC patch cords

**IP networking**

* IPv4 addressing
* `/25` subnetting
* Network addresses
* Host addresses
* Broadcast addresses
* Default gateways

**Router configuration**

* Configuring router interfaces
* Assigning IP addresses
* Enabling interfaces
* Connecting different IP networks

**DHCP**

* DHCP pools
* Network declaration
* Default gateway
* Automatic IP address assignment
* Excluding reserved addresses

**Switching**

* Connecting end devices to switches
* Basic switch configuration
* Physical network separation

**Later networking concepts introduced during discussion**

* VLANs
* Access ports
* Trunk ports
* Inter-VLAN routing
* Router-on-a-Stick
* Router subinterfaces

### 13. Overall SIWES significance

Day 83 was an important practical networking exercise because it moved beyond simply connecting devices and introduced the configuration of an actual multi-network environment.

I worked with two departmental networks using:

* Two switches
* One router
* Four PCs
* Two `/25` subnets
* DHCP

I successfully made the network work and gained practical experience with router configuration, switch configuration, subnetting, and DHCP.

The lab also provided a foundation for later topics such as **VLANs and inter-VLAN routing**, even though VLANs were not part of this particular exercise.
