DAY 86
## SIWES Day 86 — Detailed Summary

### What I originally reported

I stated that on **SIWES Day 86**, I **crimped an Ethernet cable for a biometric device that had been violently hit**.

The biometric device had apparently suffered physical impact, and its network connection/cable was affected. My practical task was to prepare and terminate an Ethernet cable so that the biometric device could be connected back to the network.

### What was inferred/explained from the activity

The activity involved a basic network-cabling repair:

1. **Identified the biometric device and its network connection**

   * The biometric/attendance device uses an Ethernet connection to communicate over the organization's LAN.
   * Since the device had been physically hit, the Ethernet cable or its connector could have been damaged or pulled loose.

2. **Prepared the Ethernet cable**

   * The damaged or unsuitable end of the Ethernet cable was cut off.
   * The outer jacket was stripped carefully to expose the internal twisted-pair wires.
   * The individual conductors were arranged in the correct Ethernet wiring order.

3. **Crimped the RJ45 connector**

   * The conductors were inserted into an RJ45 connector in the appropriate sequence.
   * The connector was placed into a crimping tool and firmly crimped so that the contacts and strain relief secured the cable.

4. **Tested the cable**

   * The newly terminated cable would normally be checked with a LAN/network cable tester to confirm that the conductors were correctly connected.
   * A successful test would indicate that the cable was properly terminated and suitable for reconnecting the biometric device.

5. **Reconnected the biometric device**

   * After the cable was confirmed to be functional, it could be connected back to the biometric device and network.
   * The device's network connectivity could then be checked to ensure that it was communicating normally.

### Technical concepts involved

This activity exposed me to:

* **Ethernet cabling**
* **RJ45 connectors**
* **Crimping and cable termination**
* **Twisted-pair network cables**
* **Network cable testing**
* **LAN connectivity**
* **Biometric/attendance devices**
* **Basic physical-layer troubleshooting**
* **Hardware damage and its effect on network connectivity**

### Main lesson

I learned that physical damage to a network-connected device or its cable can interrupt communication with the network. Properly terminating a replacement Ethernet cable is one way of restoring the physical network connection. I also gained practical experience with **crimping and terminating Ethernet cables**, rather than only learning about network cabling theoretically.

### Possible concise SIWES logbook entry

**Crimped and terminated an Ethernet cable for a physically damaged biometric device and tested the cable for proper network connectivity.**

```text
                 Site B Router
                      |

                      |

              /        |        \
```

```text
             SITE A                         SITE B

  PCs ──┤

        │                                           │
   172.20.1.0/24                              VLAN 10
                                                VLAN 20
                                                VLAN 30
```

DAY 89
**SIWES Day 89 — Detailed Summary**

### 1. Activities You Originally Reported

On **SIWES Day 89**, you reported two main activities:

#### A. Biometric Device — Ethernet Cable Recrimping

* You encountered another issue involving a **biometric device**.
* The issue required **recrimping the Ethernet/network cable**.
* This was another practical troubleshooting activity involving a biometric device and its network connectivity.
* The likely purpose of recrimping was to restore a reliable physical network connection between the biometric device and the network.
* This builds on previous SIWES activities where you had worked on biometric devices and Ethernet cables, including repairing a cable for a biometric device that had been physically damaged.

#### B. Active Directory — Trust Relationship Error

* You learnt more about **Active Directory (AD)**.
* You encountered/learnt about a particular error involving a **loss of trust between a computer system and the domain/database**.
* You remembered the issue as being related to a **password**.
* You learnt about:

  * What the error means.
  * Possible causes of the error.
  * How the problem can be diagnosed.
  * Methods that can be used to resolve the problem.
* The issue was related to the relationship between a computer/workstation and the Active Directory domain.

### 2. Technical Interpretation of the Active Directory Error

The error you were describing is most likely the Windows/Active Directory error commonly referred to as:

**“The trust relationship between this workstation and the primary domain failed.”**

This occurs when the secure trust relationship between a domain-joined computer and the Active Directory domain is broken.

A domain-joined computer has a **computer account in Active Directory**. There is also a corresponding computer-side password/secret used to establish the secure relationship between the workstation and the domain controller.

If the credentials/password information on the workstation and the information associated with the computer account in Active Directory become inconsistent, the workstation may no longer be able to authenticate properly with the domain.

### 3. Possible Causes You Learnt About

Potential causes of a broken computer/domain trust relationship include:

* The **computer account password** in Active Directory becoming different from the password/secret stored on the workstation.
* Problems involving the computer account in Active Directory.
* Restoring an old VM snapshot or system image, causing the workstation's stored domain credentials to become outdated.
* Problems with the domain controller or domain authentication.
* Improperly restoring or cloning domain-joined machines.
* Network/DNS problems preventing the workstation from communicating correctly with the domain controller.
* A computer account being reset, deleted, recreated, or otherwise changed in Active Directory while the workstation still has the previous credentials.
* Other domain authentication inconsistencies.

### 4. How the Problem Can Be Resolved

The specific resolution depends on the cause, but common approaches include:

* Checking that the computer can properly communicate with the **domain controller**.
* Checking **DNS configuration**, since Active Directory relies heavily on DNS.
* Verifying that the computer is still correctly joined to the domain.
* Resetting the computer account in Active Directory when appropriate.
* Repairing/resetting the secure channel between the workstation and the domain.
* Removing the computer from the domain and joining it again when other repair methods are unsuitable.
* Ensuring that the workstation and domain controller have appropriate credentials and network connectivity.

### 5. Connection to Your Previous Active Directory Practice

This Day 89 activity fits into the broader Active Directory work you had been doing during your SIWES.

You had been learning/practicing:

* Creating an **Active Directory/domain environment**.
* Working with a **domain controller**.
* Creating organizational structures/OUs.
* Creating users and groups.
* Connecting computers to a domain.
* Understanding how domain-joined computers communicate with the domain controller.
* Troubleshooting domain-related problems.
* Understanding that Active Directory is not simply a database of users; it manages identities, computers, groups, authentication, permissions, and other domain resources.

You had also previously worked on a practical setup involving **VirtualBox**, where you created an Active Directory environment and worked on making a domain controller accessible to another VM/computer on the network.

### 6. Skills Demonstrated/Learned on Day 89

**Networking / Hardware**

* Ethernet cable troubleshooting.
* RJ45 cable recrimping.
* Physical-layer troubleshooting.
* Biometric device network troubleshooting.
* Understanding that a damaged or poorly terminated Ethernet cable can cause network connectivity problems.

**System Administration**

* Active Directory fundamentals.
* Domain and workstation relationships.
* Computer accounts in Active Directory.
* Domain authentication.
* Secure trust relationships.
* Troubleshooting domain-joined Windows computers.

**Troubleshooting**

* Identifying symptoms of a broken domain trust.
* Considering multiple possible causes rather than assuming the problem is hardware.
* Understanding the relationship between local workstation credentials and Active Directory.
* Learning different approaches to restoring domain connectivity/authentication.

### 7. Possible SIWES Logbook Entry

A concise version suitable for the logbook would be:

**Day 89:**
Troubleshot a biometric device network issue by recrimping its Ethernet cable. Learnt about Active Directory and the “trust relationship between this workstation and the primary domain failed” error, including its possible causes and methods of resolving the issue.

### 8. Important Technical Note

Your original description said something like **“loss of trust between system and database”** and that it involved a password. The more technically accurate terminology is **loss/breakage of the trust relationship between a domain-joined workstation and the Active Directory domain**.

The “database” you remembered is likely **Active Directory**, which stores the computer account information, while the password-related part refers to the **machine account password/secure channel credentials** used for communication between the workstation and the domain.

So the key concept to preserve in your SIWES record is:

**Biometric Ethernet recrimping + Active Directory + broken workstation/domain trust relationship + machine account password mismatch + troubleshooting/resolution.**

## SIWES Day 90 — Complete Summary

* The SSD research focused on the differences between SSD types, particularly in terms of:

  * **Speed/performance**
  * **Storage capacity/size**
  * Different SSD types/form factors
* I also carried out some research on **different types of hard drives**.
* In addition, I researched **RAM**, including its types and basic differences.

### 2. Interpretation of the technical learning

The day involved both **practical hardware maintenance** and **independent technical research**.

The practical hardware work exposed me to physically installing/replacing storage and power components in a laptop. The SSD and HDD work provided practical exposure to computer storage devices, while installing the battery involved working with another replaceable laptop component.

The research component was intended to improve my understanding of computer hardware rather than simply carrying out the physical installation. In particular, I was expected to understand that SSDs can differ in their physical form factor, interface, performance, and capacity.

The SSD research could therefore cover distinctions such as:

* **SATA SSDs** — commonly use the SATA interface and are generally slower than NVMe SSDs.
* **NVMe SSDs** — use the PCIe interface and generally provide significantly higher data-transfer speeds.
* **2.5-inch SSDs** — a common physical form factor, especially in laptops and desktops designed for SATA drives.
* **M.2 SSDs** — compact SSDs that can use either SATA or NVMe depending on the specific drive and slot.
* SSD capacity can vary considerably, with larger capacities allowing more data to be stored.
* Speed is affected by the SSD's interface and technology, so two SSDs with the same capacity may have very different performance.

The HDD research involved learning that hard drives are mechanical storage devices and can differ by factors such as physical size, rotational speed, interface, capacity, and intended use.

The RAM research involved learning about memory used by the computer for temporarily holding data and instructions needed by running programs, as well as differences between RAM generations/types and capacities.

DAY 92
## SIWES Day 92 — IT Asset Inventory and Serial Number Documentation

### Original Activity Report

On SIWES Day 92, I went with the other IT students to another branch of the company to carry out an IT equipment identification and documentation exercise.

We inspected the computers and other IT equipment at the branch and recorded their identifying information. For the laptops and desktop computers, we used the Windows Command Prompt and the `wmic` command to retrieve the BIOS serial numbers. The command used was related to `wmic win32_bios` / `wmic bios get serialnumber`.

We also recorded the **display name of the users** associated with the laptops and desktop computers. This allowed us to associate each computer with its respective user during the inventory process.

In addition to the computers, we recorded the serial numbers of other equipment at the branch, including:

* Printers
* Avaya devices/phones
* Projectors

The purpose of the exercise was essentially to identify and document the organization's IT assets and their corresponding serial numbers for proper equipment records and inventory management.

### Technical Procedure

For laptops and desktop computers:

1. Opened **Command Prompt** on the Windows computer.
2. Used the Windows Management Instrumentation Command-line (`wmic`) to retrieve BIOS information.
3. Retrieved the computer's **BIOS serial number**.
4. Recorded the serial number alongside the computer/user information.
5. Recorded the user's **display name** to identify the person associated with the computer.

Other equipment was physically inspected, and their serial numbers were manually recorded:

* Printer serial numbers
* Avaya serial numbers
* Projector serial numbers

### Main Technical Concepts Encountered

**1. Computer Serial Numbers**
A computer's serial number is a unique identifier assigned to the device by its manufacturer. It can be useful for asset tracking, maintenance records, warranty identification, replacement records, and organizational inventory.

**2. BIOS Serial Number**
The BIOS/system serial number can be retrieved through Windows management tools. Using `wmic` provided a quick way to obtain this information without physically checking the manufacturer's label on every computer.

**3. WMIC**
WMIC stands for **Windows Management Instrumentation Command-line**. It provides a command-line interface for accessing information about Windows systems and hardware.

The activity demonstrated how command-line tools can be useful during IT support and asset-management tasks.

**4. User Display Name**
Recording the user's display name alongside the computer's serial number helped establish which user was associated with a particular device. This creates a clearer relationship between the organization's personnel and its assigned IT equipment.

**5. IT Asset Inventory**
The exercise was part of an IT asset inventory process. Instead of recording only computers, the inventory covered multiple categories of equipment, including computers, printers, Avaya devices, and projectors.

### Equipment Documented

The equipment identified and recorded during the exercise included:

| Equipment         | Information Recorded                              |
| ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------- |
| Laptop computers  | BIOS/system serial number and user's display name |
| Desktop computers | BIOS/system serial number and user's display name |
| Printers          | Printer serial number                             |
| Avaya devices     | Avaya serial number                               |
| Projectors        | Projector serial number                           |

### Skills Demonstrated/Gained

* Retrieving computer BIOS/system serial numbers.
* Identifying and documenting IT hardware.

* Associating computer assets with their assigned users.
* Working collaboratively with other IT students.
* Carrying out IT documentation across a company branch.

* Cleared **`%temp%` files**, which contain temporary data generated during normal system operations.

**5. Physical Computer Hardware Maintenance**

* VLAN 30 — `172.20.1.32/30`

DAY 104
**SIWES Day 104 — Detailed Summary**

On SIWES Day 104, the major activities involved the **continuation of quarterly preventive maintenance** and the **troubleshooting and restoration of an offline CCTV camera**.

### 1. Quarterly Preventive Maintenance

I continued participating in the organization's **quarterly preventive maintenance activities**. This involved carrying on with the systematic maintenance of IT equipment and related systems to help ensure that devices remained functional, reliable, and available for users.

The preventive maintenance work formed part of the broader quarterly maintenance exercise being carried out across different departments and equipment. The purpose was to identify and address potential problems before they resulted in equipment failure or disruption of operations.

### 2. Troubleshooting an Offline CCTV Camera

I also participated in the troubleshooting of a **CCTV camera that had gone offline**.

The camera was investigated to determine the cause of the loss of connectivity. During the troubleshooting process, it was discovered that the **wooden board on which the camera was mounted had become damaged/failing**. The condition of the mounting board had affected the camera installation and contributed to the camera going offline.

### 3. Cable Re-crimping

To help restore the camera's connection, the network cable associated with the camera was **re-crimped**.

This involved correcting the cable termination so that the Ethernet connection could function properly. Re-crimping was necessary because a poor or damaged termination can prevent a CCTV camera from communicating with the network and can cause it to appear offline.

### 4. PoE Splitter Installation

A **PoE splitter** was also added as part of the corrective work.

The PoE splitter was used to provide the necessary power and network connectivity arrangement for the camera. This helped address the connectivity/power requirements of the CCTV installation and supported the restoration of the camera to operational status.

### 5. Skills and Technical Experience Gained

The activities on Day 104 provided practical experience in:

* Quarterly preventive maintenance of IT equipment and systems.
* CCTV troubleshooting and fault diagnosis.
* Identifying physical infrastructure problems affecting networked devices.
* Inspecting CCTV mounting and cabling installations.
* Ethernet cable re-crimping and termination.
* Working with Power over Ethernet (PoE) equipment.
* Installing and using a PoE splitter.
* Restoring an offline network-connected CCTV camera.
* Understanding how physical damage to infrastructure can affect IT/network equipment.
* Applying systematic troubleshooting to identify and resolve connectivity problems.
* Participating in routine preventive maintenance within an organizational IT environment.

### Overall Day 104 Summary

Day 104 combined **routine preventive maintenance with practical CCTV troubleshooting**. I continued the quarterly maintenance exercise and worked on an offline CCTV camera whose installation had been affected by the deterioration/failure of its wooden mounting board. The connection was corrected by **re-crimping the cable**, and a **PoE splitter was added** to support the camera's power and network connectivity. The activity strengthened my practical experience in CCTV maintenance, Ethernet cabling, PoE technology, fault diagnosis, and preventive IT maintenance.


DAY 105
## SIWES Day 105 — Detailed Summary

### Main Activities

On SIWES Day 105, I carried out routine maintenance activities on IT equipment, including a **Canon plotter** and several **user computers**. I also participated in the troubleshooting and repair of a **CCTV camera that had gone offline**.

### 1. Canon Plotter Maintenance

I performed maintenance on the Canon plotter used in the organization. The maintenance was part of the routine IT equipment servicing carried out to ensure that the plotter remained functional and available for users who needed printing or plotting services.

The activity involved checking and maintaining the equipment to ensure proper operation and reduce the possibility of faults affecting users.

### 2. User PC Maintenance

I also carried out maintenance on additional user PCs. This involved attending to the computers used by staff and ensuring that they remained in proper working condition.

The maintenance contributed to keeping users' workstations functional and reducing issues that could affect their daily activities.

### 3. Troubleshooting of an Offline CCTV Camera

A major activity for the day involved troubleshooting a **CCTV camera that had gone offline**.

During the troubleshooting process, the camera and its network/power connection were examined to identify the cause of the problem. It was discovered that the **Ethernet transmission terminal/connection point on the camera was damaged**, preventing proper transmission through the Ethernet connection.

### 4. Camera Board Replacement

To resolve the camera fault, a **faulty/damaged board inside the camera was replaced**. The replacement was necessary to restore the camera's functionality.

After replacing the board, an additional problem with the Ethernet transmission connection was addressed.

### 5. PoE Splitter Installation

Because the **Ethernet port's transmission terminal was damaged**, a **PoE (Power over Ethernet) splitter** was added as part of the solution.

The PoE splitter provided an alternative way to handle the camera's power and network connection despite the damaged Ethernet transmission terminal. This allowed the camera to be reconnected to the network and restored to operational status.

### Technical Knowledge/Skills Demonstrated

The activities for the day provided practical experience in:

* Preventive and routine maintenance of IT equipment.
* Maintenance of Canon plotting equipment.
* User PC troubleshooting and maintenance.
* CCTV camera troubleshooting.
* Identifying hardware-related causes of network camera failure.
* Replacing faulty camera hardware/components.
* Understanding Ethernet transmission problems.
* Understanding the use of PoE equipment in CCTV/network installations.
* Restoring an offline IP camera to operational status.
* Diagnosing problems involving both hardware and network connectivity.

### Overall Summary

SIWES Day 105 involved a combination of **routine IT equipment maintenance and practical CCTV troubleshooting**. I maintained the Canon plotter and several user PCs while also troubleshooting an offline CCTV camera. The camera fault was traced to a damaged internal board and a damaged Ethernet transmission terminal. The damaged board was replaced, and a PoE splitter was added to provide a workable connection despite the damaged Ethernet terminal. This gave me practical experience in **hardware maintenance, network troubleshooting, CCTV systems, Ethernet connectivity, and PoE technology**.



DAY 106
**SIWES Day 106**

### Activity

On Day 106 of my SIWES, I worked on the **realignment and adjustment of a CCTV camera** to improve its surveillance coverage.

### Details of the Work

* I worked on a CCTV camera that had **two types of lens movement/configuration**:

  * A **fixed camera lens**, which was intended to maintain a view of a specific designated area.
  * A **rotatable lens**, which could be adjusted to view different directions or additional areas.
* The camera was **realigned and repositioned** so that its fixed lens properly covered its intended area.
* The rotatable lens was also adjusted so that it could monitor **other surrounding areas** without compromising the primary view.
* The objective was to achieve **better surveillance coverage** by allowing the camera to maintain its primary field of view while also providing visibility of other relevant locations.
* The adjustment helped ensure that important areas within the camera's surveillance range were properly visible and reduced unnecessary blind spots.

### Technical Knowledge/Skills Gained

* CCTV camera alignment and positioning.
* Understanding the difference between **fixed and rotatable camera lenses**.
* Adjusting surveillance equipment according to the required viewing area.
* Improving CCTV coverage through proper camera positioning.
* Identifying how camera orientation affects surveillance visibility and potential blind spots.

### Concise SIWES Logbook Version

**Realignment and adjustment of a CCTV camera with fixed and rotatable lenses to improve surveillance coverage. The fixed lens was positioned toward its designated area while the rotatable lens was adjusted to monitor additional areas.**

DAY 110
## SIWES Day 110 — Linux User Authorization and File Permissions

On SIWES Day 110, I carried out a practical task involving **Ubuntu Linux, user account management, file permissions, and authorization**. The exercise helped me understand how Linux can be used to control access to files and assign different levels of privileges to different users.

### Task Performed

I set up an **Ubuntu Linux machine** and created a file named **`confidential`**. The purpose of the exercise was to configure different user accounts and give each user a specific level of access to the file.

Three user accounts were configured:

1. **Ben — Administrator Access**

   * Ben was given full access to the `confidential` file.
   * Permissions: **`rwx`**
   * **Read (`r`)** — Ben can open and view the contents of the file.
   * **Write (`w`)** — Ben can modify the contents of the file.
   * **Execute (`x`)** — Ben has execute permission, where applicable.
   * This represents the highest level of access among the three users.

2. **Timothy — Operator Access**

   * Timothy was given permission to read and modify the file.
   * Permissions: **`rw`**
   * **Read (`r`)** — Timothy can view the contents.
   * **Write (`w`)** — Timothy can make changes to the file.
   * Timothy does not have execute permission.

3. **John — View-Only Access**

   * John was given read-only access to the file.
   * Permission: **`r`**
   * **Read (`r`)** — John can view the contents of the file.
   * John cannot modify or execute the file.

### Concepts Learned

The practical exercise introduced me to the way **Linux authorization and access control** work. I learned that Linux allows different users to have different permissions when accessing files and other system resources.

I learned the meaning of the basic Linux file permission symbols:

* **`r` = Read** — allows a user to view the contents of a file.
* **`w` = Write** — allows a user to modify the contents of a file.
* **`x` = Execute** — allows execution of a file when the file type and system context support execution.

The exercise also demonstrated that access does not have to be the same for every user. A sensitive file such as `confidential` can be protected by assigning privileges according to the user's responsibilities.

### Authorization and Access Control

The main lesson from the exercise was how **authorization** can be used to determine what individual users are allowed to do after they have access to a Linux system.

Instead of allowing every user unrestricted access to the file, different privileges were assigned based on their roles:

**Ben → `rwx` → Full access**
**Timothy → `rw` → Read and modify**
**John → `r` → Read only**

This provided practical understanding of the **principle of least privilege**, where users should only receive the permissions necessary for their assigned responsibilities.

### Overall SIWES Learning Outcome

Day 110 gave me practical experience with **Ubuntu Linux administration, user account configuration, file permissions, authorization, and access control**. I learned how Linux permissions can be used to protect confidential information and prevent unauthorized users from modifying or accessing resources beyond their assigned privileges.

The exercise strengthened my understanding of **Linux system administration and basic cybersecurity concepts**, particularly how user roles and permissions can be used to secure files and control access within a system.
